# 26. Sūrat al-Shuʿarāʾ

Two hundred and twenty-seven verses. Meccan. One of the sūras with the highest verse count in the Qurʾān — it comes after al-Baqara, al-Aʿrāf and Āl ʿImrān. But a large number of verses is not a large bulk: the verses of al-Shuʿarāʾ are **short**, most of them a single sentence, some of them three words (*wa jannātin wa ʿuyūn*, 134). The sūra takes its length not from the length of its sentences but from their **number**.

It takes its name from a word in **verse 224**: **ٱلشُّعَرَآء** — *al-shuʿarāʾ*, "the poets". This word occurs in the last five verses of the sūra and nowhere else in the whole of it. **The sūra therefore takes its name from its own final paragraph** — as al-Baqara takes its name from verses 67-73 and al-Naml from verse 18. This is the ordinary situation in the naming of the sūras of the Qurʾān: the name is not a summary of the subject but **a distinctive word that occurs in the sūra**.

The real subject of the sūra is not poets. The real subject of the sūra is **repetition**.

Seven prophets are recounted — Moses (Mūsā), Abraham (Ibrāhīm), Noah (Nūḥ), Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot (Lūṭ), Shuʿayb — and all seven are set into **the same mould**. Five of them open with the same sentence, eight of them (counting the opening section) close with the same two-verse seal, and within them the same two sentences are repeated eight times and five times. **These repetitions are not the ornament of the sūra but its skeleton** — and because they can be counted, they will be tabulated in a separate section of this commentary.

The sūra says three things in succession:

1. **Do not grieve** (3) — that the addressee does not believe is not a failure of the messenger.
2. **It was like this before you too** (10-191) — seven accounts, seven times the same outcome.
3. **This speech is not your invention** (192-227) — its source, its language, the place it came down, and **whose speech it is not**.

And the end of the third section attaches to the beginning of the first: the sūra opens *"perhaps you will destroy yourself"* (3) and closes *"those who did wrong will soon know to what place of return they will return"* (227). **The sūra thus begins by telling the messenger "this is not your business" and ends by saying "God will give the outcome".**

---

## The structure of the sūra

The block boundaries of the sūra are not disputed, because **the text itself draws them**. Every block ends with the same two verses.

| Block | Verses | Subject | What ends the block |
|---|---|---|---|
| **I** | 1-9 | The Book, consolation for the messenger, the possibility of compulsion, the proof from plants | **8-9** — the seal |
| **II** | 10-68 | **Moses** — the call, Pharaoh, the sorcerers, the parting of the sea | **67-68** — the seal |
| **III** | 69-104 | **Abraham** — the idols, the five-sentence sequence, the prayer, the scene of the gathering | **103-104** — the seal |
| **IV** | 105-122 | **Noah** — "the lowest of the people have followed you" | **121-122** — the seal |
| **V** | 123-140 | **Hūd** — monuments on the hills, fortresses, seizing like tyrants | **139-140** — the seal |
| **VI** | 141-159 | **Ṣāliḥ** — "will you be left secure here?", the she-camel | **158-159** — the seal |
| **VII** | 160-175 | **Lot** — "the males of all the worlds?" | **174-175** — the seal |
| **VIII** | 176-191 | **Shuʿayb** — measure, weight, not shortchanging people in their goods | **190-191** — the seal |
| **IX** | 192-213 | The source of the Qurʾān: who sent it down, in what language, who did not send it down | **213** — *fa-lā tadʿu* |
| **X** | 214-227 | Commands to the messenger and **the section on the poets** | **227** — *munqalabin yanqalibūn* |

**All eight of the eight blocks end with the same two verses.** This is an order not found at this density in any other sūra of the Qurʾān. Al-Ṣāffāt too had a sequence of prophets and a closing seal (`037-saffat.md`) — but there the seal **was in four pieces and was not stamped in full on everyone**. In al-Shuʿarāʾ the seal **is two verses and is exactly the same eight times**. A comparison of the two sūras is given below in a separate table.

### The verse-ending — the single voice of the sūra

**All two hundred and twenty-seven verses of the sūra end with a nasal letter (nūn or mīm) following a long vowel.** There are two dominant patterns: **‑īna** (*muʾminīn, ʿālamīn, ṣādiqīn*) and **‑ūna** (*yaʿlamūn, yafʿalūn, yahīmūn*); the minority that comes between them ends in **‑īm** (*al-raḥīm, ʿaẓīm, ʿalīm, ḥamīm*).

| Verse-ending | Example | Where it is dense |
|---|---|---|
| **‑īna** | *muʾminīn* (8), *al-ʿālamīn* (109), *al-mursalīn* (105) | The whole sūra |
| **‑ūna** | *taʿbudūn* (70), *yahīmūn* (225), *yafʿalūn* (226) | In the speech sections |
| **‑īm(i)** | *al-raḥīm* (9, 68, 104, 122, 140, 159, 175, 191), *ʿaẓīm* (135, 156, 189), *ʿalīm* (220) | **The second verse of the seal** |

**What is worth recording is this:** the second verse of the seal (*wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*) **departs** from the dominant sound of the sūra — it ends not in *‑īn* but in *‑īm*. Eight blocks undergo the same change of sound at the same place eight times. **The seal is therefore stamped not only with a word but with a sound:** the reader hears that the block has ended.

**I record this as a fact; I do not enter into any reckoning of letters or numbers** — that road is closed in this commentary (`002-bakara.md`). What is recorded is an observation about sound of the same kind as those recorded in the sections on al-Qalam and al-Ṣāffāt (`068-kalem.md`, `037-saffat.md`).

### The occasion of revelation and the historical place

On the sūra's being Meccan there is something close to unanimity. **Some sources transmit that the final verses (224-227, or 227 alone) are Medinan**; the ground given is that the expression *wa'ntaṣarū min baʿdi mā ẓulimū* ("and they defended themselves after being wronged") in verse 227 points to the permission to defend oneself. **This is a transmitted view; I adopt no firm preference.**

The sūra as a whole sits inside the central dispute at Mecca and answers three of the claims made in it:

| Claim | The sūra's answer | Where |
|---|---|---|
| "This man is possessed / a poet" | What poets do is described; the conduct described is not the conduct of the messenger | **224-226** |
| "The satans are bringing this to him" | *Wa mā tanazzalat bihi'sh-shayāṭīn* — whom they do come down upon is stated | **210-212, 221-223** |
| "Let a miracle come to us and we will believe" | *In nashaʾ nunazzil ʿalayhim mina's-samāʾi āyatan* — what would happen if one came is stated | **4** |

The basmala was treated in earlier sections; the verses are numbered without it.

---

## The backbone of the sūra — seven accounts, four moulds

**Every table in this section is a count of the text. It is not interpretation; where something is interpretation it is marked as such.**

From verse 10 to verse 191 of the sūra — that is, **one hundred and eighty-two verses** — seven prophetic accounts are recounted. Four of the accounts sit inside a shared frame:

1. **The notice of denial:** *Kadhdhabat … al-mursalīn*
2. **The opening:** *Idh qāla lahum akhūhum … alā tattaqūn*
3. **The threefold introduction:** *innī lakum rasūlun amīn · fa'ttaqū'llāha wa aṭīʿūn · wa mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajrin…*
4. **The closing seal:** *inna fī dhālika la-āyatan… · wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*

**And all four elements of the frame are missing in the accounts of Moses and Abraham.** This is the most striking structural datum in the sūra, and it will be treated separately below.

### Table 1 — The closing seal: eight occurrences

> *إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَءَايَةً ۖ وَمَا كَانَ أَكْثَرُهُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ · وَإِنَّ رَبَّكَ لَهُوَ ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلرَّحِيمُ*
>
> *Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan wa mā kāna aktharuhum muʾminīn · Wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*
>
> "In this there is surely a sign; but most of them were not believers. · And your Lord — He indeed — is the Mighty, the Merciful."

| # | Verses | Which block it closes | Is there an addition before the seal |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **8-9** | **The opening** (1-9) | none |
| 2 | **67-68** | **Moses** (10-68) | none |
| 3 | **103-104** | **Abraham** (69-104) | none |
| 4 | **121-122** | **Noah** (105-122) | none |
| 5 | **139-140** | **Hūd** (123-140) | **yes** — *fa-kadhdhabūhu fa-ahlaknāhum* |
| 6 | **158-159** | **Ṣāliḥ** (141-159) | **yes** — *fa-akhadhahumu'l-ʿadhāb* |
| 7 | **174-175** | **Lot** (160-175) | none |
| 8 | **190-191** | **Shuʿayb** (176-191) | none |

**Six of the eight occurrences are bare; in two of them (Hūd and Ṣāliḥ) a short sentence announcing destruction has been added at the head of the first verse of the seal.** This is a difference that can be verified from the text.

**The reason is not stated in the text. I record an observation as my own reading, and it is not binding:** in the accounts of Noah (119-120), Lot (170-173) and Shuʿayb (189) the destruction **has already been recounted in the verses before the seal**; in the accounts of Hūd and Ṣāliḥ it has not — the account of Hūd ends with the people's words *"we are not going to be punished"* (138), and the account of Ṣāliḥ with *"they became regretful"* (157). **The addition at the head of the seal therefore looks as though it is completing information that was left missing.** This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### Table 2 — The opening mould: five accounts, two missing

> *كَذَّبَتْ … ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · إِذْ قَالَ لَهُمْ أَخُوهُمْ … أَلَا تَتَّقُونَ*

| Prophet | Notice of denial | Verb | Is *akhūhum* present | *alā tattaqūn* |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Moses** | **none** | — | **none** | **11** — *alā **yattaqūn*** (third person) |
| **Abraham** | **none** | — | **none** | **none** |
| **Noah** | **105** — *kadhdhabat qawmu Nūḥin* | **كَذَّبَتْ** (feminine) | **106** — *akhūhum Nūḥ* ✔ | **106** ✔ |
| **Hūd** | **123** — *kadhdhabat ʿĀdun* | **كَذَّبَتْ** (feminine) | **124** — *akhūhum Hūd* ✔ | **124** ✔ |
| **Ṣāliḥ** | **141** — *kadhdhabat Thamūdu* | **كَذَّبَتْ** (feminine) | **142** — *akhūhum Ṣāliḥ* ✔ | **142** ✔ |
| **Lot** | **160** — *kadhdhabat qawmu Lūṭin* | **كَذَّبَتْ** (feminine) | **161** — *akhūhum Lūṭ* ✔ | **161** ✔ |
| **Shuʿayb** | **176** — *kadhdhaba aṣḥābu'l-Aykati* | **كَذَّبَ** (masculine) | **177** — ***none*** | **177** ✔ |

**There are three separate facts in this table, and all three are read off the text:**

**One — in the accounts of Moses and Abraham there is no opening mould.** Both begin with a different sentence: Moses with *wa idh nādā rabbuka Mūsā* (10), Abraham with *wa'tlu ʿalayhim nabaʾa Ibrāhīm* (69). **Neither begins with "the people denied".**

**Two — in the account of Moses the sentence *alā tattaqūn* is present, but its person has changed.** In verse eleven: *qawma Firʿawn; **alā yattaqūn*** — "[go] to the people of Pharaoh; **will they not guard themselves?**" **The sentence is therefore spoken not by the prophet to those he addresses, but by God to the prophet, and those addressed become the third person.** In the other five accounts the same sentence is in the form *tattaqūn* (you), and it is **the prophet's own speech**.

**Three — in the account of Shuʿayb there is no *akhūhum* ("their brother").** In four of the five accounts the prophet is named as the "brother" of his people; with Shuʿayb this drops away and only *idh qāla lahum Shuʿaybun* is said. **The reason appears in how those addressed are named:** in the other four they are named by a **lineage** (*qawmu Nūḥ*, *ʿĀd*, *Thamūd*, *qawmu Lūṭ*); here by a **place**: *aṣḥābu'l-Ayka* — "the people of al-Ayka", that is, those who dwell in the dense thicket. **One is the brother of a lineage; one is not called the brother of the people of a place.**

**And the same difference is reflected in the verb as well:** because the words *qawm*, *ʿĀd* and *Thamūd* can be treated as feminine in Arabic, the verb is *kadhdhabat*; because *aṣḥāb* is a masculine plural, the verb is *kadhdhaba*. **The two differences in verse 176 — the dropped *akhūhum* and the changed verb — can therefore be traced to the same cause. I record this as an observation about the structure.**

### Table 3 — The threefold introduction: identical in five accounts

| Sentence | Noah | Hūd | Ṣāliḥ | Lot | Shuʿayb | Moses | Abraham |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| *Innī lakum rasūlun amīn* | **107** | **125** | **143** | **162** | **178** | none | none |
| *Fa'ttaqū'llāha wa aṭīʿūn* | **108** | **126** | **144** | **163** | **179** | none | none |
| *Wa mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajrin…* | **109** | **127** | **145** | **164** | **180** | none | none |

**In all five of the five accounts these three sentences come immediately after the opening verse and in the same order.** The first four verses of each account (denial + opening + threefold introduction) are thus identical with one another; the accounts diverge **from the fifth verse onward**.

**This is the most easily verifiable structural datum in the sūra:** the ranges 105-109, 123-127, 141-145, 160-164 and 176-180 — five blocks of five verses — are the same **but for a single word** (the name of the prophet). With Shuʿayb one further word drops (*akhūhum*).

### Table 4 — *Fa'ttaqū'llāha wa aṭīʿūn*: eight occurrences

The sentence occurs once inside the introductory mould in each of the five accounts; **in three accounts it is repeated once more**.

| Prophet | First occurrence (introductory mould) | Second occurrence | What lies between |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Noah** | **108** | **110** | **109** — that he asks no wage |
| **Hūd** | **126** | **131** | **128-130** — three offences: the monument, the fortress, seizing like tyrants |
| **Ṣāliḥ** | **144** | **150** | **146-149** — the favours and the carving of houses out of mountains |
| **Lot** | **163** | none | — |
| **Shuʿayb** | **179** | none | — |

**Eight occurrences in all: 108, 110, 126, 131, 144, 150, 163, 179.**

**What is worth recording is what precedes the second occurrence.** With Noah a single verse comes between. With Hūd and Ṣāliḥ what comes between is **a list enumerating the deeds of the people** — and when the list ends the same sentence is repeated. **The sentence therefore frames the list:** "guard yourselves against God and obey me → [you are doing these things] → guard yourselves against God and obey me."

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the position of the sentence in the three accounts.** In the accounts of Lot and Shuʿayb there is no repetition; there the list of offences is attached directly to the call itself (165-166 and 181-183), and no distance comes between that would require framing.

### Table 5 — *Wa mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajrin*: five occurrences

> *وَمَآ أَسْـَٔلُكُمْ عَلَيْهِ مِنْ أَجْرٍ ۖ إِنْ أَجْرِىَ إِلَّا عَلَىٰ رَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ*
>
> "I ask you no wage for it; my wage falls only upon the Lord of all the worlds."

| Prophet | Verse |
|---|---|
| **Noah** | 109 |
| **Hūd** | 127 |
| **Ṣāliḥ** | 145 |
| **Lot** | 164 |
| **Shuʿayb** | 180 |

**All five are exactly the same — there is not a letter of difference.** The Qurʾān has counterparts of this sentence in other sūras as well (Hūd 11/29, 11/51; Sabaʾ 34/47; Yā Sīn 36/21 — *ittabiʿū man lā yasʾalukum ajran*). **What is distinctive in al-Shuʿarāʾ is that the sentence is repeated five times in the same sūra and in the same position.**

**The function of this sentence is visible across the whole sūra:** the sūra describes what the messenger is and what he is not. **A man who asks no wage cannot be explained by interest.** And at the end of the sūra, describing the poets, it will say *"they say what they do not do"* (226) — **the fit between speech and conduct is thus the measure of the sūra from beginning to end.** I record this as an observation about the context.

### Al-Shuʿarāʾ and al-Ṣāffāt — two sequences of prophets

**The same structure was treated in `037-saffat.md`, and the frame set out there is not repeated here.** The comparison of the two sūras is this:

| | **Al-Ṣāffāt 37/75-148** | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/10-191** |
|---|---|---|
| **How many prophets** | 6 (Noah, Abraham, Moses-Aaron, Elijah, Lot, Jonah) | **7** (Moses, Abraham, Noah, Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Lot, Shuʿayb) |
| **How many pieces to the seal** | **4 sentences** (*taraknā · salām · najzi'l-muḥsinīn · min ʿibādina'l-muʾminīn*) | **2 verses** |
| **Is the seal stamped on everyone** | **No** — with Lot and Jonah there is not one piece | **Yes** — eight out of eight |
| **The opening mould** | Three separate forms + three occurrences of *wa inna … la-mina'l-mursalīn* | *Kadhdhabat … al-mursalīn* + *idh qāla lahum akhūhum* |
| **Prophets in common** | Noah, Abraham, Moses, Lot | Noah, Abraham, Moses, Lot |
| **Only there** | Elijah, Jonah, Isaac | **Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb** |
| **The content of the seal** | **Praise** — peace and recompense | **Outcome** — "most did not believe" |

**The two sūras use the same technique for two different ends.** The seal of al-Ṣāffāt looks toward **the prophet**: peace is given to him, recompense is promised to him. The seal of al-Shuʿarāʾ looks toward **the people**: *wa mā kāna aktharuhum muʾminīn* — "most of them did not believe".

**And this difference separates what the two sūras say to the one addressed. I record it as my own reading:** al-Ṣāffāt says "the same recompense will be given to you"; **al-Shuʿarāʾ says "the same answer will be given to you"**. The third verse of al-Shuʿarāʾ (*laʿallaka bākhiʿun nafsaka*) supports this reading — the sūra opens with the grief of the messenger.

**And the three prophets found only in al-Shuʿarāʾ (Hūd, Ṣāliḥ, Shuʿayb) belong to the Arabian peninsula's own history.** ʿĀd, Thamūd and Midian/al-Ayka are names known in the geography of those addressed. The sequence of al-Ṣāffāt, by contrast, is built out of names from the tradition of the Children of Israel. **This is a difference that can be read off the text; I issue no ruling.**

---

## 26/1-2 — طسٓمٓ · تِلْكَ ءَايَٰتُ ٱلْكِتَٰبِ ٱلْمُبِينِ

*Ṭā-sīn-mīm · Tilka āyātu'l-kitābi'l-mubīn*

"Ṭā-sīn-mīm. · **These are the verses of the clear Book.**"

### The disconnected letters

**The question has been set out many times across the series** (`002-bakara.md`, `036-yasin.md`, `038-sad.md`, `050-kaf.md`, `068-kalem.md`) **and is not repeated.** Its summary: no certain knowledge about the meaning of these letters has been transmitted; the most honest position is to say that it is not known.

**The only thing to be added here is a datum from the text:** the letters *ṭā-sīn-mīm* occur in the Qurʾān **at the head of two sūras** — al-Shuʿarāʾ (26) and al-Qaṣaṣ (28). Sūrat al-Naml, which lies between them, begins with *ṭā-sīn*. **Three consecutive sūras — 26, 27, 28 — thus open with the same family of letters**, and all three recount the account of Moses. This is an overlap that can be counted.

**The opening of al-Qaṣaṣ was treated in this commentary** (`028-kasas.md` 28/1-6), and the same note was entered there.

### تِلْكَ — the demonstrative that points to the distant

**تِلْكَ** — feminine, the demonstrative pronoun used for **the distant** ("that", "there it is"). For the near one would say *hādhihi*.

**A point of word order worth recording:** the verses are in the hand of the one addressed, they are being recited — that is, they are near. **Yet the distant demonstrative is used.** The Arabic grammarians explain this usage as **exaltation** (*taʿẓīm*): the pronoun that points to the distant announces **the elevated rank** of what is pointed to.

**The same opening is repeated in several sūras of the Qurʾān:** *tilka āyātu'l-kitābi'l-ḥakīm* (Yūnus 10/1), *tilka āyātu'l-kitābi'l-mubīn* (Yūsuf 12/1), *tilka āyātu'l-kitābi'l-ḥakīm* (Luqmān 31/2 — treated in `031-lokman.md`). **Al-Shuʿarāʾ uses the same attribute as Yūsuf: *al-mubīn*.**

### ٱلْمُبِين — root ب-ي-ن

**The root has been analysed in many places across the series** (`043-zuhruf.md` 43/2, `044-duhan.md` 44/2, `036-yasin.md`) **and is not repeated.** The description given there: at the core of the root lies **separation, the opening of a gap between two things**; *bayn* — the space between. A thing becomes visible when it **separates out**.

**And *mubīn* is an active participle**, that is, not "made clear" but **"making clear"**. The distinction matters here: the Book is not merely intelligible, it is **what makes things intelligible**.

**A link within the sūra:** the same word occurs four more times in the sūra:

| Verse | Construction | What it qualifies |
|---|---|---|
| **2** | *al-kitābi'l-mubīn* | **The Book** |
| **32** | *thuʿbānun mubīn* | What the staff turned into |
| **97** | *ḍalālin mubīn* | Error |
| **115** | *nadhīrun mubīn* | Noah himself |
| **195** | *lisānin ʿarabiyyin mubīn* | **The language** |

**The sūra thus opens with the word for clarity and, in verse 195, ties that same word to language.** This link will be treated in the section on 192-199.

---

## 26/3 — لَعَلَّكَ بَٰخِعٌ نَّفْسَكَ أَلَّا يَكُونُوا۟ مُؤْمِنِينَ

*Laʿallaka bākhiʿun nafsaka allā yakūnū muʾminīn*

"**You are almost destroying yourself because they will not believe.**"

### بَاخِع — root ب-خ-ع

**The root is analysed here for the first time in this commentary.**

**The concrete sense the lexicographers give for the root ب-خ-ع comes from the slaughter of animals:** *bakhaʿa'sh-shāta* — in slaughtering a sheep, to carry the knife **as far as the point at the very base of the neck vertebrae called the *bukhʿ***. The cut has been taken past what was necessary, carried **to the very end of the limit**.

**Two derived senses arise from this:**

| Derivative | Sense | Shared core |
|---|---|---|
| *Bakhaʿa nafsahu* | To drive oneself as though to destruction | **To carry to the end** |
| *Bakhaʿa bi'l-ḥaqqi* | To render what is due in full, without shortfall | **To carry to the end** |

**At the centre of the root, then, lies not "excess" but "reaching the final point".** Reaching the final point in slaughter wounds the animal beyond need; reaching the final point in rendering what is due gives it in full. **The same motion produces two different results in two different contexts.**

**And the picture of the verse comes from this:** the messenger is carrying his grief **as far as his own life**. The word does not say "to grieve greatly"; it says **"to carry grief to the point where life ends"**.

**That an active participle is used must also be recorded:** *bākhiʿun* — "destroying". A noun, not a verb (*tabkhaʿu*). **In Arabic the active participle can announce that the action is a settled state in the agent.** What is described is therefore not a passing grief but **a continuing condition**.

**لَعَلَّ** — usually rendered "perhaps, it may be that". **Here it announces no expectation; the lexicographers give this usage the sense of *ishfāq* (anxiety, warning against).** The closest English renderings are "almost" and "you are on the point of".

### أَلَّا يَكُونُوا۟ مُؤْمِنِينَ — the cause

*Allā yakūnū* — originally *li-allā yakūnū*, that is, **a construction announcing cause:** "**because** they will not believe".

**There is a point of word order that must be recorded:** the verse does not give as the cause of the grief the **hostility**, the **injury** or the **mockery** of those addressed. All of these occur elsewhere in the sūra (29, 116, 167, 186). **The only cause given is *that they do not believe***.

### The same limit — the seventh entry in the series

**This verse is one of the sharpest statements in the Qurʾān of the limit drawn for the messenger, and it has been treated six times across the series.** In `028-kasas.md` 28/56 these verses were gathered into a table. **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/3 is the seventh row added to that table:**

| Place | Expression | What it limits |
|---|---|---|
| Al-Aḥqāf 46/35 | *Balāgh* | **The task** — it is delivery |
| Al-Zumar 39/41 | *Wa mā anta ʿalayhim bi-wakīl* | **Responsibility** |
| Qāf 50/45 | *Wa mā anta ʿalayhim bi-jabbār* | **The authority to compel** |
| Fāṭir 35/8 | *Fa-lā tadhhab nafsuka ʿalayhim ḥasarāt* | **Grief** |
| Luqmān 31/23 | *Fa-lā yaḥzunka kufruh* | **Grief** |
| Al-Qaṣaṣ 28/56 | *Lā tahdī man aḥbabta* | **Love** — the closest bond |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/3** | ***Laʿallaka bākhiʿun nafsaka*** | **The body** — life itself |

**Seven verses, seven different words.** And that of al-Shuʿarāʾ stands on the same line as that of Fāṭir, but one step beyond it:

| | **Fāṭir 35/8** | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/3** |
|---|---|---|
| **Mood** | **Prohibition** — *fa-lā tadhhab* ("let it not go") | **Statement** — *laʿallaka bākhiʿun* ("you are almost doing it") |
| **Object** | *Nafsuka* — your life | *Nafsaka* — your life |
| **Verb** | *Tadhhab … ḥasarāt* — to waste away in regret | *Bakhaʿa* — to cut to the very end |
| **What it says** | **"Do not"** | **"You are doing it"** |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the difference of mood:** in Fāṭir there is a prohibition, in al-Shuʿarāʾ **an observation**. Fāṭir holds the messenger back from a behaviour; al-Shuʿarāʾ **shows him the condition he is already in**. Since it is not known with certainty which of the two verses came down first, I set up no ordering.

### What this says to the present

The condition the verse describes is one that everyone who takes on a task recognises: **bearing the weight of an outcome one cannot change.** The verse does not blame this condition — the word *bākhiʿun* is not the name of a fault, and the rest of the sūra does not scold the messenger. **The verse only shows the limit:** the work done is **delivery**; what the one who receives it will do belongs to another domain.

**And the fourth verse, coming immediately after, says why this is so.**

---

## 26/4 — إِن نَّشَأْ نُنَزِّلْ عَلَيْهِم مِّنَ ٱلسَّمَآءِ ءَايَةً فَظَلَّتْ أَعْنَٰقُهُمْ لَهَا خَٰضِعِينَ

*In nashaʾ nunazzil ʿalayhim mina's-samāʾi āyatan fa-ẓallat aʿnāquhum lahā khāḍiʿīn*

"**If We willed, We would send down upon them a sign from heaven, and their necks would stay bowed before it.**"

### The work of the sentence

The verse is **a conditional sentence**, and it announces that the condition is not realised. What it says is this: **compulsion is possible; but it is not being done.**

**And this is the answer to the grief in the third verse.** The messenger grieves that those addressed do not believe. The verse says: **making them believe is not a matter that stands at the limit of power.** If it were willed, the necks would bow. **It is not willed** — and this is not a lack but a **choice**.

### فَظَلَّتْ — the work of the perfect verb

**ظَلَّ** — one of the sisters of *kāna*; its original sense is **"to remain in a state through the day"**, and from there it widens to **"to be continuously in that state"**. *Fa-ẓallat* is in the perfect.

**A point of word order:** the apodosis of a conditional (*jawāb al-sharṭ*) normally comes in the imperfect — *fa-taẓallu* would have been expected. **That it comes in the perfect is the Arabic technique of recounting as already done what is certain to happen.** Not "they would bow", then, but **"they stayed bowed"**. The condition has not been realised; but the certainty of the apodosis is announced by the mood of the verb.

**And the sense of the verb *ẓalla* itself is added here:** the necks do not bow for a moment, they **stay bowed**. Continuance lies inside the verb.

### أَعْنَٰقُهُمْ لَهَا خَٰضِعِينَ — the famous grammatical question

**This construction is one of the disputed places in Arabic grammar, and the dispute is this:**

*Aʿnāq* (necks) is **a plural of what has no reason**; by rule its predicate should have come as a feminine singular or a broken plural: *khāḍiʿatan* or *khāḍiʿatun*. **But the word that comes is *khāḍiʿīn* — the sound masculine plural used for rational beings.**

**I give the transmitted explanations in a table; I do not impose a preference:**

| Explanation | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|
| **The neck has stood in place of its owner** | In Arabic a part can represent the whole; for the neck to bow is already for the person to bow | It explains the result, not why the rule was broken |
| **The predicate belongs not to the necks but to their owners** (*hum khāḍiʿīna*) | A hidden subject is supplied in the sentence | It requires a supplied word |
| **The necks have been treated as human** | The Qurʾān can give the rational plural pattern to inanimate things (Yūsuf 12/4 — *sājidīn* for the stars) | The intra-Qurʾānic example is strong but limited |
| ***Aʿnāq* means "a body, the leading men"** | Some lexicographers transmit that the word *ʿunuq* means "the leading men of a people" | This sense is not widespread |

**The example of Yūsuf 12/4 is worth recording:** there, of eleven stars, it is said *raʾaytuhum lī sājidīn* — the pattern for rational beings. **Where a thing performs the act of bowing or prostrating, then, the Qurʾān can use the language of rational beings for it.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

### Why the neck was chosen

**عُنُق** — the neck. **The neck is the place where submission and resistance appear at the same time.** The head stands upright because the neck carries it; the one who submits bends his neck. This is why the word *aʿnāq* occurs in Arabic in contexts both of pride (*shāmikhu'l-ʿunuq* — his neck held high) and of bondage (*fī aʿnāqihim aghlālan* — Yā Sīn 36/8).

**And there is a link within the sūra:** in Yā Sīn 36/8 collars are put around the necks and the heads are forced upward; here the necks bow **of themselves**. **In both verses the neck is brought into a position outside the will.** I record this as an observation.

### The possibility of compulsion and the choice against it

**The distinction the verse states is briefly this: possibility and act are not the same thing.**

| | **Possibility** | **Act** |
|---|---|---|
| The verse's expression | *In nashaʾ nunazzil* | The condition is not realised |
| Its result | The necks bow | The necks do not bow |
| What it announces | **Power is unlimited** | **A method has been chosen** |

**The Qurʾān sets up this distinction elsewhere too:** *wa law shāʾa rabbuka la-āmana man fi'l-arḍi kulluhum jamīʿā* (Yūnus 10/99), *wa law shiʾnā la-ātaynā kulla nafsin hudāhā* (al-Sajda 32/13 — treated in `032-secde.md`). **All three verses have the same structure: willing + compelling + not realised.**

**And the difference here is worth recording:** in Yūnus and al-Sajda the instrument of the compulsion is not named; **in al-Shuʿarāʾ it is: a sign coming down from heaven.** The sūra thus says that the very thing those addressed are asking for (a miracle) would in fact **be compulsion**.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the description of the result:** *khāḍiʿīn* — to bow. The verse recounts what would happen if a miracle came not as "they would believe" but as **"their necks would bow"**. **The difference between faith and bowing the neck stands in the verse's choice of word.** This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### What this says to the present

There are two ways of getting a view accepted: **making it irresistible** and **persuading**. The first is quick, its result is certain, and what it produces is **a bowed neck**. The second is slow, its result is uncertain, and what it produces is **conviction**. The verse says that the first is possible and chooses the second — and it says this to a messenger who is grieving.

---

## 26/5-6 — وَمَا يَأْتِيهِم مِّن ذِكْرٍ مِّنَ ٱلرَّحْمَٰنِ مُحْدَثٍ إِلَّا كَانُوا۟ عَنْهُ مُعْرِضِينَ · فَقَدْ كَذَّبُوا۟ فَسَيَأْتِيهِمْ أَنۢبَٰٓؤُا۟ مَا كَانُوا۟ بِهِۦ يَسْتَهْزِءُونَ

*Wa mā yaʾtīhim min dhikrin mina'r-raḥmāni muḥdathin illā kānū ʿanhu muʿriḍīn · Fa-qad kadhdhabū fa-sa-yaʾtīhim anbāʾu mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn*

"**No new reminder comes to them from the Merciful but that they turn away from it.** · So they have denied; and the tidings of what they used to mock will come to them before long."

### مُحْدَث — "new"

**Root ح-د-ث**: to come to be afterwards, to occur. *Ḥadīth* — what is new, an event, a saying. **مُحْدَث** — passive participle: **brought into being afterwards, newly sent down**.

**The word is an attribute of *dhikr* and looks to the moment of revelation:** each newly descending portion. The verse therefore recounts not a single event but **a repeated reaction**: **each time a new one comes, the same thing happens.**

**It is known that this word has been drawn into the debate in the history of theology over whether the Qurʾān is "created".** In this commentary I do not enter that debate; the context of the verse is **the newness of the revelation**, not a dispute about being. **I record this as a limitation, not as a view.**

### ٱلرَّحْمَٰن — the choice of the name

**The name was analysed in detail in `055-rahman.md` and is not repeated.**

**What is to be added here is an observation about the structure:** the name set opposite the act of turning away is not *al-Qahhār* or *al-Jabbār* but **al-Raḥmān**. **What is turned away from is therefore presented not as a threat but as a mercy.** The same technique was recorded at `036-yasin.md` 36/11 and `043-zuhruf.md` 43/36.

### فَقَدْ كَذَّبُوا۟ — from past to future

**The shift of tense between the two verbs is worth recording:**

| Verb | Mood | Time |
|---|---|---|
| *Kadhdhabū* | Perfect | **It happened** |
| *Sa-yaʾtīhim* | Imperfect + *sīn* | **It will happen** |

**سَ** (*sīn*) announces the near future in Arabic. For the distant future *sawfa* is used. **Not "one day", then, but "before long".**

**أَنۢبَآء** — the plural of *nabaʾ*; root **ن-ب-أ**: **a great and weighty piece of news.** The root was analysed in `078-nebe.md`. Ordinary news is called *khabar*; weighty news, *nabaʾ*.

**And the point of word order is this: what is to come is not the punishment but *the tidings*.** *Anbāʾu mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* — "the tidings of what they used to mock". **When the thing mocked comes about, then, it no longer arrives as tidings but as event.** This expression is repeated in the Qurʾān: *wa ḥāqa bihim mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* (al-Anʿām 6/10; Hūd 11/8).

---

## 26/7 — أَوَلَمْ يَرَوْا۟ إِلَى ٱلْأَرْضِ كَمْ أَنۢبَتْنَا فِيهَا مِن كُلِّ زَوْجٍ كَرِيمٍ

*A-wa lam yaraw ila'l-arḍi kam anbatnā fīhā min kulli zawjin karīm*

"**Do they not look at the earth? How many a noble kind have We caused to grow in it.**"

### The place of the proof

**In the fourth verse a sign that would come down from heaven was spoken of. The seventh verse points to the sign standing on the earth.**

| | **26/4** | **26/7** |
|---|---|---|
| From where | *Mina's-samāʾ* — from heaven | *Fi'l-arḍ* — on the earth |
| Its state | **It does not come down** (the condition is not realised) | **It is already there** |
| Its effect | It makes the neck bow | **It requires looking** |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the sequence of the two verses:** the sign that is asked for does not come, because **the sign that stands is not looked at**. *A-wa lam yaraw* — "have they not looked?"

### ٱلْأَرْض and إِلَىٰ

**A point of word order:** the verb is *lam yaraw* (they did not see), but it comes with the preposition *ilā*: *yaraw **ilā** al-arḍ*. **In Arabic *raʾā* takes a direct object (*raʾā'sh-shayʾa* — he saw the thing); when it comes with *ilā*, the sense of looking, of directing the gaze, comes to the front.**

**The verse therefore says not "did they not see?" but "did they not look?"** Seeing is a passive business; it happens while the eye is open. **Looking is a directed business.** The difference stands in the preposition.

### زَوْج — root ز-و-ج

**The root was analysed at `078-nebe.md` 78/8 (*wa khalaqnākum azwājā*) and at `043-zuhruf.md` 43/12 (*khalaqa'l-azwāja kullahā*).**

The description given there: **زَوْج** is not "a pair" but **"one member of a pair"**. Anything that has a mate is called *zawj*. The English "pair" names two things together; the Arabic *zawj* names **the single thing that has a mate**.

**Its use for plants is worth recording:** the verse names plants as *zawj*. The Qurʾān repeats this in several places (*min kulli'th-thamarāti jaʿala fīhā zawjayni'thnayn* — al-Raʿd 13/3; *wa anbatnā fīhā min kulli zawjin bahīj* — Qāf 50/7, treated in `050-kaf.md`).

**That plants mate (pollination, male and female organs) is a fact known today.** No claim of a scientific miracle is set up in this commentary — **the use of the word in Arabic already covers everything that has a mate, and in some plants such as the date palm the distinction of male and female was something the Arab farmer knew.** What is to be recorded is this: **the verse names the thing it asks to be looked at not as "variety" but as "mating".** I leave this as an observation about the word.

### كَرِيم — of a plant

**Root ك-ر-م**: **generosity, worth, nobility.** Used of a person the word means "worthy, noble"; used of soil, **"fertile"**; used of a plant, **"useful, fine"**.

**The lexicographers give the core of the word as "a thing's being the good of its own kind".** What is *karīm* is what gives more than it had to give.

**And the same word will occur once more later in the sūra:** *wa maqāmin karīm* (58) — the "noble station" taken from the people of Pharaoh. **The sūra thus gives the same attribute both to what the earth gives and to what man loses.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.

---

## 26/8-9 — إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَءَايَةً ۖ وَمَا كَانَ أَكْثَرُهُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ · وَإِنَّ رَبَّكَ لَهُوَ ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلرَّحِيمُ

*Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan wa mā kāna aktharuhum muʾminīn · Wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*

"**In this there is surely a sign; but most of them were not believers.** · **And your Lord — He indeed — is the Mighty, the Merciful.**"

### The first stamping of the seal

**The seal that will occur eight times in the sūra occurs here for the first time — and here it does not yet close an account.** What it closes is **a proof**: the plant of the seventh verse.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is position:** in the sūra the seal comes first after a **proof from nature**; in the seven occurrences that follow it will come after a **proof from history**. **The sūra thus applies the same judgement to two different kinds of proof:** "in this there is a sign, most did not believe" — for the plant as well as for the seven peoples.

### وَمَا كَانَ أَكْثَرُهُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ — mood and quantity

**Two things must be recorded.**

**One — *mā kāna* is in the perfect.** The imperfect (*wa mā aktharuhum muʾminīn*) could also have been said; and indeed the Qurʾān says it so elsewhere (Hūd 11/17 — *fa-lā taku fī miryatin minh*; Yūsuf 12/103 — *wa mā aktharu'n-nāsi wa law ḥaraṣta bi-muʾminīn*). **Here the perfect has been chosen: "were not believers."** This accords with the accounts to be recounted in the seven occurrences that follow — there it will really be the past that is spoken of.

**Two — that *akthar* (most) is said is that *kull* (all) is not said.** The seal is repeated eight times, and in all eight it says "most". **That a minority believed in every account is thus hidden in the sentence itself.** In the account of Noah this minority also appears in the text (*wa man maʿiya mina'l-muʾminīn*, 118); in the account of Lot, his household (170); in the account of Ṣāliḥ it does not appear.

**I record this as an observation about the structure:** the sentence does not announce despair. **What it announces is a proportion.**

### ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلرَّحِيم — the two names coming together

**Both names have been analysed across the series and are not repeated:** for *ʿazīz*, `035-fatir.md` and `059-hasr.md`; for *raḥīm*, `055-rahman.md` and `001-fatiha.md`.

In summary: the core of the root **ع-ز-ز** is **hardness, resistance to wear** — *arḍun ʿazāz*, hard ground. The *ʿazīz* is the one who cannot be reached, cannot be overcome, cannot be broken. The core of the root **ر-ح-م** is **the womb** — the closeness that protects and raises.

**What is to be added here is their coming together.** This pair occurs in about thirteen places in the Qurʾān, and **the eight occurrences in al-Shuʿarāʾ make up the greater part of that.** The instances outside the sūra: Yā Sīn 36/5 (*tanzīla'l-ʿazīzi'r-raḥīm*), al-Rūm 30/5, al-Sajda 32/6, al-Dukhān 44/42.

**The work of the pair is visible in the context of the sūra:**

| Name | What it looks toward | Its counterpart in the sūra |
|---|---|---|
| **al-ʿAzīz** | Irresistibility | The end of the seven peoples — those destroyed |
| **al-Raḥīm** | Protecting and watching over | The end of the seven prophets — those saved |

**The second verse of the seal thus gives both faces at once of the judgement "most did not believe" in the first verse.** And this explains why the sūra closes every account with **two verses**: **one verse states the fact, the other the measure.**

**And the pronoun is worth recording: *la-huwa*.** *Wa inna rabbaka **la-huwa** al-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm* — there is a separating pronoun (*ḍamīr al-faṣl*) between them. **In Arabic this pronoun announces restriction (*ḥaṣr*) and emphasis: "the truly Mighty and Merciful is He."** The same construction does not occur at the beginning of the sūra, nor at its end — **only in this seal, eight times.**

---

## The account of Moses — 26/10-68 (introduction)

**The longest section of the sūra: fifty-nine verses.** About one third of the total length of the seven accounts.

**And the account does not fit the mould of the sūra:** there is no *kadhdhabat … al-mursalīn* opening, no *idh qāla lahum akhūhum*, no *innī lakum rasūlun amīn* triad. **There is only the closing seal** (67-68).

**In the Qurʾān the account of Moses is recounted in many sūras, and each sūra brings a different piece to the front.** In this commentary the section `028-kasas.md` treated the part of the account running **from the birth to Midian and the call in the valley**; `079-naziat.md` 79/15-26 treated **the shortest telling** (twelve verses). **The part that falls to al-Shuʿarāʾ is what comes after the call: the presence of Pharaoh, the sorcerers, and the sea.**

| Sūra | What it brings to the front | Where it was treated |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Qaṣaṣ 28** | The birth, the mother, Midian, the call | `028-kasas.md` |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26** | **The confrontation with Pharaoh, the sorcerers, the sea** | here |
| **Al-Nāziʿāt 79** | The shortest summary — *idhhab ilā firʿawna innahu ṭaghā* | `079-naziat.md` |
| **Al-Ṣāffāt 37** | Only the deliverance and the victory — four verses | `037-saffat.md` |
| **Al-Dukhān 44** | The abandoning of the people, the transfer of the inheritance | `044-duhan.md` |

---

## 26/10-11 — وَإِذْ نَادَىٰ رَبُّكَ مُوسَىٰٓ أَنِ ٱئْتِ ٱلْقَوْمَ ٱلظَّٰلِمِينَ · قَوْمَ فِرْعَوْنَ ۚ أَلَا يَتَّقُونَ

*Wa idh nādā rabbuka Mūsā ani'ti'l-qawma'ẓ-ẓālimīn · Qawma Firʿawn; alā yattaqūn*

"**And when your Lord called Moses: 'Go to the wrongdoing people — the people of Pharaoh. Will they still not guard themselves?'**"

### The scene of the call is not recounted here

**The first thing to be recorded is an absence:** al-Shuʿarāʾ does not recount **here** the valley, the fire, the tree, the staff or the hand. It begins straight away with the command: *ani'ti'l-qawma'ẓ-ẓālimīn.*

**The same scene was recounted in detail at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/29-35 and was treated in `028-kasas.md`;** in Ṭāhā 20/9-24 it occurs in its longest form. **What al-Shuʿarāʾ leaves out is what the others recount.**

**I record this as an observation:** the sūra does not recount the account from the beginning; it takes it up **at the point where it enters the mould**. And the mould of the sūra is this: **the messenger speaks, the people answer.** In the scene in the valley there is no people.

### أَنِ ٱئْتِ — the bareness of the command

**أَن** here is explicative (*tafsīriyya*): it **opens the content** of the verb *nādā* (he called) that precedes it. It is like saying "he called thus:".

**And the command is a single word: *iʾti* — go.** Neither a consolation nor an equipment is set beside it. **The equipment will not come until the fifteenth verse** (*fa'dhhabā bi-āyātinā*). The order is therefore this: **first the command, then the objection, then the support.**

### ٱلْقَوْمَ ٱلظَّٰلِمِينَ · قَوْمَ فِرْعَوْنَ — from attribute to name

**A point of word order worth recording.** Those addressed are named twice, and the order is this:

| Order | Expression | What it gives |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *al-qawma'ẓ-**ẓālimīn*** | **The attribute** — what they do |
| 2 | *qawma **Firʿawn*** | **The identity** — who they are |

**What they are is said first, then who they are.** The attribute has been brought forward, the name comes after.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order:** the reason for the mission is not identity but **deed**. The same technique was recorded in `089-fecr.md` too where the peoples are named — the Qurʾān targets a community not by its name but by its **attribute**. This is the textual ground of the principle recorded in `STYLE.md`: the judgement is built not on an ethnic or religious group but on the **attribute**.

### أَلَا يَتَّقُونَ — the mould of the sūra, in the reversed person

**The sentence *alā tattaqūn*, which will occur five times in the sūra, occurs here for the first time — and in this one place its person is different: *alā yattaqūn*.**

| Account | Verse | Who says it | To whom | Person |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Moses** | **11** | **God** | **To Moses** | ***yattaqūn*** — third person |
| Noah | 106 | Noah | To his people | *tattaqūn* — second person |
| Hūd | 124 | Hūd | To his people | *tattaqūn* |
| Ṣāliḥ | 142 | Ṣāliḥ | To his people | *tattaqūn* |
| Lot | 161 | Lot | To his people | *tattaqūn* |
| Shuʿayb | 177 | Shuʿayb | To his people | *tattaqūn* |

**The sentence therefore occurs six times; in five it is the prophet's address to his people, in one it is God's word to the prophet.** This is a difference that can be verified from the text.

**I record an explanation as my own reading, and it is not binding:** in the other five accounts the messenger is from within his own people — *akhūhum*, "their brother". Moses is not from within the people he is sent to; **he goes to the people of Pharaoh from outside.** That the sentence comes in the third person accords with that distance: **the people are not yet close enough to be addressed as "you".**

**And there is a datum from the text that supports this explanation:** the same people will say to Moses, in the eighteenth verse, *"did we not raise you?"* **Moses grew up among that people but is not of them.** The sūra preserves this doubleness.

**أَلَا** — *hamza* (interrogative) + *lā* (negation). In Arabic the pattern announces not a question but **urging and reproach**: "still not?"

---

## 26/12-14 — قَالَ رَبِّ إِنِّىٓ أَخَافُ أَن يُكَذِّبُونِ · وَيَضِيقُ صَدْرِى وَلَا يَنطَلِقُ لِسَانِى فَأَرْسِلْ إِلَىٰ هَٰرُونَ · وَلَهُمْ عَلَىَّ ذَنۢبٌ فَأَخَافُ أَن يَقْتُلُونِ

*Qāla rabbi innī akhāfu an yukadhdhibūn · Wa yaḍīqu ṣadrī wa lā yanṭaliqu lisānī fa-arsil ilā Hārūn · Wa lahum ʿalayya dhanbun fa-akhāfu an yaqtulūn*

"He said: '**My Lord! I fear that they will deny me. · My breast grows tight and my tongue does not loosen; so send to Aaron (Hārūn) as well. · Moreover they hold an offence against me; I fear that they will kill me.**'"

### Three objections — their order

**The command was a single sentence. The answer is three verses.** And the order of the three objections is worth recording:

| Order | Objection | What it belongs to |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *An yukadhdhibūn* — they will deny | **The other side's reaction** |
| 2 | *Yaḍīqu ṣadrī · lā yanṭaliqu lisānī* | **His own condition** |
| 3 | *Lahum ʿalayya dhanbun · an yaqtulūn* | **His past and the danger to his life** |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order:** the lightest first, the heaviest last. The fear of death is named third — **not first.** The text therefore gives the messenger's foremost anxiety as **the failure of the mission**, not as his own life.

### وَيَضِيقُ صَدْرِى — the tightening of the breast

The root **ض-ي-ق**: **narrowness.** *Ḍayq* — to be narrow. Its opposites are *saʿa* (breadth) and *sharḥ* (to split open and widen).

**And the Qurʾān sets up this pair separately for Moses and for Muhammad:**

| Person | Expression | Where | Direction |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Moses** | *Wa yaḍīqu ṣadrī* — my breast grows tight | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/13** | **Tightening** — complaint |
| **Moses** | *Rabbi'shraḥ lī ṣadrī* — expand my breast | Ṭāhā 20/25 | **Expansion** — request |
| **Muhammad** | *A-lam nashraḥ laka ṣadrak* — did We not open your breast | Al-Inshirāḥ 94/1 | **Expansion** — already given |
| **Muhammad** | *Wa laqad naʿlamu annaka yaḍīqu ṣadruka bimā yaqūlūn* | Al-Ḥijr 15/97 | **Tightening** — known |

**This word was analysed in `094-insirah.md`, and the description recorded there holds here too.** What is to be added here is an overlap: **the Qurʾān records that the breast of both messengers grew tight; to one it says "We expanded it", the other asks "expand it".**

**And a link can be made with the third verse of the sūra. I record it as my own reading:** at 26/3 it was said to the one addressed (Muhammad) *"you are almost destroying yourself"*. **In the thirteenth verse another messenger says "my breast grows tight".** Twenty verses lie between the two, and both recount **the burden the messenger carries**. That this link is made is not stated in the text; the position offers an overlap.

### وَلَا يَنطَلِقُ لِسَانِى — the tongue not loosening

The root **ط-ل-ق**: **the untying of a bond, becoming free.** *Ṭalāq* (the untying of the bond of marriage) is from the same root — it was treated in `065-talak.md`. *Inṭilāq* (Form VII) — **to come untied of itself, to be released.**

**The expression therefore does not say "I cannot speak".** It says: **my tongue does not loosen** — it stays tied, it does not flow.

**And the Qurʾān recounts the same condition in Ṭāhā with another word:** *wa'ḥlul ʿuqdatan min lisānī* (20/27) — "untie a knot from my tongue". **The two sūras use the same picture: bond and knot.**

**Reports are transmitted to the effect that this condition had a medical or physiological cause; the Qurʾān gives no cause.** The only thing the text gives is **the condition itself**: the tongue does not flow. About the cause I say nothing.

**And Moses' own words at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/34 confirm this:** *wa akhī Hārūnu huwa afṣaḥu minnī lisānā* — "my brother Aaron is clearer than I in tongue". **That passage was treated in `028-kasas.md`, and I rely on that treatment.**

### فَأَرْسِلْ إِلَىٰ هَٰرُونَ — the word order of the request

**A point of word order worth recording.** Moses does not say *"send Aaron with me"*; he says *fa-arsil **ilā** Hārūn* — "**send to Aaron**".

**The object of the verb is not stated, only its direction is given.** The transmitted explanations: (a) "send Gabriel to Aaron", that is, **reveal to him too**; (b) "give the messengership to Aaron as well". **Both explanations come out at the same door: the request is not for an assistant but for a fellow messenger.**

**The request at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/34 is different:** *fa-arsilhu maʿiya ridʾan yuṣaddiqunī* — "send him with me as a **support**, to confirm me". **As was recorded in `028-kasas.md`, there the request is for confirmation.** Two different faces of the same event are given in the two sūras: in al-Shuʿarāʾ **messengership**, in al-Qaṣaṣ **support**. I record this as an observation; it is not a contradiction but two different emphases.

### وَلَهُمْ عَلَىَّ ذَنۢبٌ — "they hold an offence against me"

**This expression refers to a piece of the account that al-Shuʿarāʾ does not recount: Moses' killing of a man in Egypt with his fist.**

**The event was treated in detail at `028-kasas.md` 28/14-21, and I rely on that treatment.** The notes there were, in summary, these: the act is not a deliberate killing (*wakaza* — to strike with the fist; *fa-qaḍā ʿalayh* — he finished him); Moses himself assesses the event (*hādhā min ʿamali'sh-shayṭān*, *innī ẓalamtu nafsī*); and the text does not cover it up.

**What is to be added here is the word order of the expression.**

**لَهُمْ عَلَىَّ ذَنۢبٌ** — word for word: **"they have [a claim concerning] an offence upon me."**

| Element | What it announces |
|---|---|
| *Lahum* | **In their favour** — they are the creditor side |
| *ʿAlayya* | **Against me** — I am the debtor side |
| *Dhanbun* | **Indefinite** — an offence |

**Moses therefore recounts the event not in the language of his own conscience but in the language of the other side's law:** "they have a claim upon me". He does not say *innī ẓalamtu nafsī* (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/16); he says **"in their eyes I am guilty"**.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the two prepositions (*li* and *ʿalā*):** the sentence is not a confession of sin but **a statement of risk**. The matter is not the weight of the offence but **what the other side will do about it** — and the next sentence says this plainly: *fa-akhāfu an yaqtulūn.*

**And the word *dhanb*:** the root **ذ-ن-ب**, whose concrete sense is **tail** (*dhanab*). The connection the lexicographers give: an offence is what a person **drags behind him** — it comes after him, it does not let go. From this picture the word widens to "a burden, an act whose consequence trails after it".

**And this picture agrees with the word order of the verse:** Moses left Egypt years before; **the offence has come after him.**

### What the three objections have in common

**All three are real, and not one of them is denied.** The answer in the fifteenth verse does not say that the objections are wrong; **it announces the outcome.** This will be treated in the next section.

---

## 26/15-17 — قَالَ كَلَّا ۖ فَٱذْهَبَا بِـَٔايَٰتِنَآ ۖ إِنَّا مَعَكُم مُّسْتَمِعُونَ · فَأْتِيَا فِرْعَوْنَ فَقُولَآ إِنَّا رَسُولُ رَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · أَنْ أَرْسِلْ مَعَنَا بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ

*Qāla kallā; fa'dhhabā bi-āyātinā; innā maʿakum mustamiʿūn · Fa'tiyā Firʿawna fa-qūlā innā rasūlu rabbi'l-ʿālamīn · An arsil maʿanā banī Isrāʾīl*

"He said: '**No! Go, both of you, with Our signs; We are with you, listening. · Come to Pharaoh and say: We are the messenger of the Lord of all the worlds. · Send the Children of Israel with us.**'"

### كَلَّا — the place of the refusal

**كَلَّا** in Arabic announces **firm refusal and deterrence**. In the Qurʾān it generally comes to reject a claim (it was treated in `075-kiyame.md`, `080-abese.md`, `102-tekasur.md`).

**What it rejects here has been disputed.** There are two readings, and both can be supported from the text:

| Reading | What it rejects | Its ground |
|---|---|---|
| **It rejects the fear** | "They will not kill you" | The guarantee comes immediately after: *innā maʿakum* |
| **It rejects the objection** | "These excuses do not lift the mission" | The command is repeated after it: *fa'dhhabā* |

**The two readings do not exclude one another.** What is to be recorded is this: the first word after *kallā* is **a command**, not a consolation. The consolation is placed behind the command.

### فَٱذْهَبَا — the dual

**The verb is dual: *idhhabā* — "go, the two of you".** But Aaron's being commissioned had not been stated in a verse; Moses had asked for it (13). **The answer therefore does not announce in a separate sentence that the request is granted — it announces it directly through the form of the verb.**

**This is a point of word order and is worth recording:** it is not said "I have sent Aaron too"; **the verb shifts to the dual.** The granting is inside the grammar.

### إِنَّا مَعَكُم مُّسْتَمِعُونَ — the pronoun shifting to the plural

**And immediately after, the pronoun changes: *maʿakum* — not dual but plural ("with you").**

**This change has been disputed. The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| **Pharaoh is included** | The exchange is to be mutual; the party listened to numbers three |
| **Two persons have been counted as a plural** | Using the plural in place of the dual is ordinary in Arabic |
| **Moses, Aaron and the Children of Israel are meant** | The Children of Israel are named in the seventeenth verse |

**I do not impose a preference.** But I record an observation: **the same verse holds both the dual (*idhhabā*) and the plural (*maʿakum*); the change is therefore a deliberate shift, not a casualness.**

**مُّسْتَمِعُون** — root **س-م-ع**, active participle of Form X. *Samiʿa* is to hear, *istamaʿa* is **to lend an ear, to listen**. **The difference matters: hearing is passive, listening is a directed business.**

**The guarantee is therefore not "we hear you" but "we are listening to you".** And this will meet, later in the sūra, the sentence that comes out of Pharaoh's mouth: *alā tastamiʿūn* (25) — "are you not listening?" **The same verb, in two different mouths.**

### إِنَّا رَسُولُ رَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ — singular "messenger", dual "we"

**This construction is one of the notable places in Arabic grammar:** the subject is plural (*innā* — we), the predicate singular (*rasūlu* — messenger). **It is not "we are messengers" (*rusulu*) but "we are the messenger".**

**The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| ***Rasūl* is in the sense of a verbal noun** | In Arabic *rasūl* is also used in the sense of "message, what is sent"; a verbal noun makes no singular-plural distinction |
| **The message of the two is one** | Two messengers carry a single messengership |
| **In Arabic *rasūl* can be both singular and plural** | The lexicographers record this property of the *faʿūl* pattern (like *ʿaduww*) |

**The Qurʾān's own usage supports the first explanation:** not *fa-inna ʿalayka laʿnatī*, but at Ṭāhā 20/47, for the same event, it says *innā **rasūlā** rabbik* (dual). **The same scene is thus given in two sūras in two different moulds: singular in al-Shuʿarāʾ, dual in Ṭāhā.** This is a difference that can be verified from the text and requires an explanation; I have given the transmitted explanations above, and I do not impose a preference.

### أَنْ أَرْسِلْ مَعَنَا بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ — that the request is a single sentence

**This is the whole of the message to be delivered to Pharaoh: one sentence.**

**And the content of the request is worth recording:** faith is not asked for, the abandoning of the throne is not asked for. **The only thing asked for is the release of a community.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the bareness of the sentence:** the messengership first demands **the lifting of a concrete wrong**. The call to God's oneness will come later (23-28), because Pharaoh asks. **The order is therefore this: first the demand of a right, then — upon the questioner's own question — the description of God.**

---

## 26/18-19 — قَالَ أَلَمْ نُرَبِّكَ فِينَا وَلِيدًا وَلَبِثْتَ فِينَا مِنْ عُمُرِكَ سِنِينَ · وَفَعَلْتَ فَعْلَتَكَ ٱلَّتِى فَعَلْتَ وَأَنتَ مِنَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ

*Qāla a-lam nurabbika fīnā walīdan wa labithta fīnā min ʿumurika sinīn · Wa faʿalta faʿlataka'llatī faʿalta wa anta mina'l-kāfirīn*

"He said: '**Did we not raise you among us as a child? Did you not stay among us many years of your life? · And then you did that deed of yours which you did; you are one of the ungrateful.**'"

### The kind of the answer

**Pharaoh does not discuss the request.** He does not say a word about whether the Children of Israel will be released, about whether the messengership is true, about who God is. **He turns the subject to the person.**

**There are two moves, and both are on the same line:**

| Move | Expression | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *A-lam nurabbika fīnā walīdā* | **Obligation** — "we looked after you" |
| 2 | *Wa faʿalta faʿlataka'llatī faʿalta* | **A past offence** — "you are a killer" |

**I record this as an observation:** neither is an argument; both are **a positioning directed at the person.** They carry the conversation from a dispute about a right to an accounting of debt and crime.

### نُرَبِّكَ — root ر-ب-و / ر-ب-ب

**The verb is *rabbā* (Form II): to raise, to bring up.** And the lexicographers record that this verb comes from the same semantic field as the word **رَبّ** (Lord): the core of the root **ر-ب-ب** is **to bring a thing up to its completion, to own it and watch over it**. The root was analysed in `001-fatiha.md`.

**And the point of word order stands here:** Moses had said he was the messenger of *rabbu'l-ʿālamīn* (16). **Pharaoh reminds him of his own nurture.** The two words are from the same root.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the sequence of the two verses:** against the claim of "the Lord of all the worlds", Pharaoh puts forward **his own lordship** — not openly, but through a verb: "I raised you". Later in the sūra he will say it openly as well (29 — *la-ini'ttakhadhta ilāhan ghayrī*).

### وَلِيدًا — infancy

The root **و-ل-د**: to give birth. *Walīd* — **newly born, a small child.** The word increases the weight of the obligation: the one looked after was in no condition to defend himself.

**And this refers to the scene at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/8-9:** the child had reached Pharaoh's palace by water, and his wife had taken him in. **That event was treated in `028-kasas.md`.** Pharaoh names the **result** of the event and does not name its **cause**: he does not say why the child was put into the water.

**Moses will recall exactly this in the twenty-second verse.**

### فَعْلَتَكَ ٱلَّتِى فَعَلْتَ — the deed that is not named

**A point of word order worth recording.** Pharaoh does not name the event: he does not say "you killed a man". **He repeats the word with a verbal noun from its own root:** *faʿalta faʿlata-ka'llatī faʿalta* — "you did that deed of yours which you did".

| Element | Word | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| Verb | *faʿalta* | You did |
| Cognate accusative | *faʿlata-ka* | That deed of yours |
| Relative clause | *allatī faʿalta* | Which you did |

**The same root three times.** In Arabic this construction announces **that the act is something known and needing no naming**. The closest English rendering: **"you did that deed of yours."**

**فَعْلَة** — the *faʿla* pattern announces **a single occurrence** (in Arabic, *maṣdar al-marra*). That is: "that one act of yours". **The same pattern was also recorded in this commentary at `054-kamer.md` 54/36 for *baṭsha*** (*baṭshatanā* — "our one seizing").

**And one further observation:** the vagueness of the expression increases the force of the accusation. An unnamed offence stands heavier than a named one — because, not being described, it cannot be answered.

### وَأَنتَ مِنَ ٱلْكَٰفِرِينَ — the sense of the word here

**The word means both "ungrateful" and "denier"; the core of the root ك-ف-ر is covering over**, and it has been analysed across the series (`109-kafirun.md`, `074-muddessir.md`).

**The context here brings the ingratitude side forward:** the sentence is the continuation of the sentence "we looked after you". **What is being said is "you covered over the kindness".**

**But the second sense is also possible, and it is transmitted:** having left the religion of Pharaoh. **Both senses can be supported from the text; I do not impose a preference.** What is to be recorded is this: **the word comes out of the mouth of the accuser** — and the sūra nowhere uses this word of Moses.

---

## 26/20-22 — قَالَ فَعَلْتُهَآ إِذًا وَأَنَا۠ مِنَ ٱلضَّآلِّينَ · فَفَرَرْتُ مِنكُمْ لَمَّا خِفْتُكُمْ فَوَهَبَ لِى رَبِّى حُكْمًا وَجَعَلَنِى مِنَ ٱلْمُرْسَلِينَ · وَتِلْكَ نِعْمَةٌ تَمُنُّهَا عَلَىَّ أَنْ عَبَّدتَّ بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ

*Qāla faʿaltuhā idhan wa anā mina'ḍ-ḍāllīn · Fa-farartu minkum lammā khiftukum fa-wahaba lī rabbī ḥukman wa jaʿalanī mina'l-mursalīn · Wa tilka niʿmatun tamunnuhā ʿalayya an ʿabbadta banī Isrāʾīl*

"He said: '**I did it then, and I was one of those who had strayed. · So I fled from you when I feared you. Then my Lord bestowed judgement upon me and made me one of the messengers. · And that favour you hold over me is that you have enslaved the Children of Israel!**'"

### The structure of the answer

**Three verses do three pieces of work, and their order is worth recording:**

| Verse | Work | Kind |
|---|---|---|
| 20 | *Faʿaltuhā idhan* | **Admission** — he does not deny the act |
| 21 | *Fa-farartu … fa-wahaba lī rabbī* | **Narrative** — what happened in between |
| 22 | *Wa tilka niʿmatun tamunnuhā* | **Counter-move** — the obligation refuted |

**Moses therefore takes up the two accusations in reverse order:** Pharaoh had named the obligation first, then the offence; Moses first admits the offence, then refutes the obligation. **The heavier is taken up first.**

### فَعَلْتُهَآ إِذًا — admission without denial

**The act is admitted. It is not covered over, not made light of; it is not called an accident.**

**إِذًا** — "then, at that time". An adverb of time. **The work of the word is to place the act in a period:** in the state I was in then.

**وَأَنَا۠ مِنَ ٱلضَّآلِّينَ** — a circumstantial clause: "while I was one of those who had strayed."

The root **ض-ل-ل**: **to lose the way, to fail to find the right direction.** The root was analysed in `001-fatiha.md` and in `053-necm.md`. **In this context the word means not "denier" but "one who does not fully know what he is doing, one who has missed the mark"** — indeed at al-Ḍuḥā 93/7 the same root is used of a messenger (*wa wajadaka ḍāllan fa-hadā*), and that distinction was treated in `093-duha.md`.

**The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| **"At that moment I was in a state of not knowing what to do"** | The sense of *ḍalāl* as "to be at a loss, to miss the mark" |
| **"Messengership had not yet been given, the knowledge was not complete"** | The continuation of the verse: *fa-wahaba lī rabbī ḥukman* |
| **"I acted without knowing the ruling"** | It accords with his own assessment at al-Qaṣaṣ 28/15-16 |

**All three point in the same direction: that the act is not owned as a deliberate murder, but is not covered over either.** As was recorded in `028-kasas.md`, the text does not conceal a grave event in the life of a prophet.

### فَفَرَرْتُ مِنكُمْ لَمَّا خِفْتُكُمْ — the confession of fear

The root **ف-ر-ر**: **to flee, to move away in order to escape.**

**A point of word order:** Moses conceals neither his flight nor his fear. **And the order is worth recording:** *farartu* (I fled) comes first, *khiftukum* (I feared you) after — the cause is placed behind the result. In Arabic *lammā* makes this link: "I fled because I feared".

**And this ties back to the objection in the fourteenth verse:** Moses had said to God *"I fear that they will kill me"*. **Now he says the same fear to the face of the one he feared.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

### فَوَهَبَ لِى رَبِّى حُكْمًا — the transformation in between

**Three verbs, three stages:** *farartu* (I fled) → *wahaba lī* (He bestowed on me) → *jaʿalanī* (He made me).

**حُكْم** — root **ح-ك-م**: **to make a thing firm, to prevent its going wrong.** The concrete use of the root comes from the keeping of animals: *ḥakama* — to put the bit into a horse's mouth; *ḥakama* is also the name of the bit itself. **Judgement, then, is what holds in check.** The root was analysed at `031-lokman.md` 31/12 (*ḥikma*).

**What is transmitted about the sense of the word here: knowledge, prophethood, the capacity to decide rightly.** I adopt no firm preference; **the core of the root (to make firm) fits all three.**

**And the same word will occur once more in the account of Abraham:** *rabbi hab lī ḥukman* (83). **Two prophets in the sūra thus use the same word — one says "it was given", the other asks "give".** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.

### وَتِلْكَ نِعْمَةٌ تَمُنُّهَا عَلَىَّ — "the favour you hold over me"

**One of the densest sentences in the sūra; it carries a whole account in a single verse.**

**تَمُنُّهَا** — root **م-ن-ن**: **to cut, to sever** (the concrete sense); from there **to bestow a gift** and **to hold a gift over someone's head**. The root was treated in `047-muhammed.md` and at `049-hucurat.md` 49/17 (*yamunnūna ʿalayka an aslamū*).

**The two faces of the word must be recorded:**

| Face | Sense | Example in the Qurʾān |
|---|---|---|
| **As a verb (of God)** | To grant a favour | *Wa nurīdu an namunna* — al-Qaṣaṣ 28/5 |
| **As a verb (of a human)** | To throw what one gave in another's face | *Lā tubṭilū ṣadaqātikum bi'l-mann* — al-Baqara 2/264 |

**And the point of word order stands exactly here:** in saying "we raised you" Pharaoh is claiming a **favour**. In his answer Moses accepts the word (*niʿmatun*) but changes the verb: ***tamunnuhā*** — "which you hold over me". **That it was a favour is not denied; what is said is what it is that stops a favour from being a favour.**

### أَنْ عَبَّدتَّ بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ — the core of the sentence

**عَبَّدْتَّ** — root **ع-ب-د**, Form II (*faʿʿala*): **to enslave, to reduce to bondage.** The core of the root is **submission, obedience**; *ʿabd* — a slave, a servant. Form II adds transitivity and **compulsion**: "you made them slaves".

**In this pattern the word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

### The grammar of the sentence — three readings

**How the verse is constructed has been disputed, and the dispute changes the meaning. I give the transmitted readings in a table; I do not impose a preference.**

| Reading | The construction of the sentence | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Interrogative of denial** (an implied question) | "Is this the favour you hold over me — that you enslaved the Children of Israel?" | **Mockery and rejection** |
| **Statement of cause** | "That favour exists only because you enslaved the Children of Israel" | **The source of the favour is wrongdoing** |
| **Full acceptance, in the reverse direction** | "Yes, this is a favour: you raised me — because you were killing all the children" | **Mockery (*istihzāʾ*)** |

**What the three readings have in common is this, and it can be read from the text with certainty:** *niʿma* and *ʿabbadta* stand in the same sentence, the one as the explanation of the other. **Moses ties the kindness done to him to the order out of which that kindness came.**

### The link with *istiḍʿāf* — al-Qaṣaṣ 28/4-5

**The background needed to understand this sentence was treated at `028-kasas.md` 28/1-6, and I rely on that treatment.** The notes there were, in summary, these:

- **يَسْتَضْعِف** — root **ض-ع-ف**, Form X: **to count someone weak and to make him weak.** The pattern matters: **weakness is not something that happens of itself but something that is done.**
- Pharaoh's work is recounted with three verbs, and the first is **dividing** (*wa jaʿala ahlahā shiyaʿā*); wrongdoing first breaks a society into pieces.
- Two verbs are set side by side: *yudhabbiḥu abnāʾahum wa yastaḥyī nisāʾahum* — to slaughter the sons, to keep the women alive.

**Now let us set two words side by side:**

| | **Al-Qaṣaṣ 28/4** | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/22** |
|---|---|---|
| **Word** | *Yastaḍʿifu* — Form X | *ʿAbbadta* — Form II |
| **Core of the root** | *Ḍaʿf* — weakness | *ʿAbd* — servitude |
| **What the pattern adds** | **Counting weak and making weak** | **Making a slave by force** |
| **Who says it** | **The narrator** — the Qurʾān, from outside | **Moses** — face to face |
| **Tense** | Imperfect — it is going on | Perfect — done, completed |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the two patterns:** in al-Qaṣaṣ the fact is **recounted**, in al-Shuʿarāʾ the fact is **said to the face**. And the word used in al-Shuʿarāʾ is the heavier: *istiḍʿāf* is to render powerless; *taʿbīd* is to reduce to bondage. **The second is the outcome the first has reached.**

**And when the two sūras are read together the whole sentence emerges:** the reason Moses grew up in the palace is that the sons of the Children of Israel were being killed. **What Pharaoh calls a "favour" is thus a by-product of his own wrongdoing — the child was put into the water because of that very order.** Al-Shuʿarāʾ does not state this chain openly; **it becomes visible when read together with al-Qaṣaṣ.** I record this link as a reading of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān.

### What this says to the present

The measure the verse sets up is a concrete measure for relations of obligation: **the worth of a kindness is measured not on its own but together with the order out of which it came.** When an order first leaves a community powerless and then does a kindness to one of that community, what emerges is **not a kindness but another face of the same order**.

**And the verse is striking in that the one who says this is not a bystanding victim but someone who actually received that kindness.** Moses does not refuse the kindness — **he carries the reckoning back to the order the giver built with his own hand.**

---

## 26/23-28 — قَالَ فِرْعَوْنُ وَمَا رَبُّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · قَالَ رَبُّ ٱلسَّمَٰوَٰتِ وَٱلْأَرْضِ وَمَا بَيْنَهُمَآ ۖ إِن كُنتُم مُّوقِنِينَ · قَالَ لِمَنْ حَوْلَهُۥٓ أَلَا تَسْتَمِعُونَ · قَالَ رَبُّكُمْ وَرَبُّ ءَابَآئِكُمُ ٱلْأَوَّلِينَ · قَالَ إِنَّ رَسُولَكُمُ ٱلَّذِىٓ أُرْسِلَ إِلَيْكُمْ لَمَجْنُونٌ · قَالَ رَبُّ ٱلْمَشْرِقِ وَٱلْمَغْرِبِ وَمَا بَيْنَهُمَآ ۖ إِن كُنتُمْ تَعْقِلُونَ

*Qāla Firʿawnu wa mā rabbu'l-ʿālamīn · Qāla rabbu's-samāwāti wa'l-arḍi wa mā baynahumā in kuntum mūqinīn · Qāla li-man ḥawlahu alā tastamiʿūn · Qāla rabbukum wa rabbu ābāʾikumu'l-awwalīn · Qāla inna rasūlakumu'lladhī ursila ilaykum la-majnūn · Qāla rabbu'l-mashriqi wa'l-maghribi wa mā baynahumā in kuntum taʿqilūn*

"Pharaoh said: '**And what is the Lord of all the worlds?**' · [Moses] said: '**The Lord of the heavens and the earth and what is between them — if you would know for certain.**' · [Pharaoh] said to those around him: '**Are you not listening?**' · [Moses] said: '**Your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers.**' · [Pharaoh] said: '**This messenger of yours who has been sent to you is certainly possessed.**' · [Moses] said: '**The Lord of the east and the west and what is between them — if you would use reason.**'"

### Six verses, six "he said"

**This is one of the most tightly built dialogue scenes in the Qurʾān.** Six verses, six *qāla*, no explanation of any kind. The narrator does not step in: who felt what, who thought what, is not said. **There are only the words.**

**And the words set up a pattern:**

| Verse | Who | What he does |
|---|---|---|
| 23 | Pharaoh | **Question** — *wa mā rabbu'l-ʿālamīn* |
| 24 | Moses | **Answer 1** — place: the heavens, the earth, what is between |
| 25 | Pharaoh | **Escape from the answer** — he turns to the listeners |
| 26 | Moses | **Answer 2** — time: you and your forefathers |
| 27 | Pharaoh | **Accusation** — *la-majnūn* |
| 28 | Moses | **Answer 3** — direction: east, west, what is between |

**The pattern is exact: Pharaoh speaks three times and all three times drops the question; Moses answers three times and all three times answers the same question.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the arrangement of the six verses:** the scene shows not a debate but **how a debate is broken off**. Pharaoh does not refute the answer — **he reacts to it.**

### وَمَا رَبُّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ — the particle of the question

**A point of word order worth recording, and one that has been disputed:** the question is asked with **مَا** ("what"), not with **مَنْ** ("who").

**In Arabic *mā* asks after quiddity (what a thing is, its kind), *man* after identity.** Rational beings are asked after with *man*.

| Particle | What it asks | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **مَنْ** | Identity | "Who is he?" |
| **مَا** | Quiddity, kind | "What is it?" |

**The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Ground | Its weak side |
|---|---|---|
| **Belittling and mockery** | *Mā* is used for the non-rational; the question asks after a thing | In Arabic *mā* is also used to ask after an attribute |
| **He is really asking after quiddity** | Pharaoh may have met a concept he did not know | Pharaoh's own claim to divinity does not fit with such innocence |
| **He wants to show that it cannot be defined** | When the kind of a thing is asked for, what has no kind cannot be defined | Interpretation |

**And Moses' answer seems to settle this dispute. I record it as my own reading:** Moses gives no quiddity. **He gives an act.** He does not say "it is of such a kind"; **he says "He is the Lord of such and such".** The question looks for a kind; the answer shows **a work**.

### The widening circle of the three answers

**Moses answers three times, and all three are in the same mould: *rabbu … wa … wa mā baynahumā*.** But the field they cover changes:

| Answer | Verse | Extent | What kind of proof | Conditional clause |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **24** | Heavens, earth, what is between | **Place** — all of existence | *In kuntum **mūqinīn*** |
| 2 | **26** | You and your forefathers | **Time** — all of history | **(no condition)** |
| 3 | **28** | East, west, what is between | **Direction** — the whole horizon | *In kuntum **taʿqilūn*** |

**The shared structure of the three answers and their differences can be verified from the text. I enter three notes:**

**One — in the second answer there is no conditional clause.** The first and the third carry a qualification ending in *in kuntum…*; the second does not. **And the second answer is the only one that takes those addressed themselves as its subject:** *rabbukum wa rabbu ābāʾikumu'l-awwalīn.* **I record it as my own reading:** a person's own existence and the existence of his forefathers are not something he needs to be persuaded of — **it is already in front of him.**

**Two — the contents of the conditions differ: *īqān* (certain knowledge) and *ʿaql* (the use of reason).** The first answer looks to the whole of the universe and sets **certain knowledge** as its condition; the third answer looks to east and west, that is, to **an order seen by the eye every day**, and sets **the use of reason** as its condition. **The larger claim is thus tied to the heavier condition, the more visible proof to the lighter one.** I record this as an observation about the structure.

**Three — the answers do not narrow backwards; they descend toward the horizon.** While the heavens and the earth are abstract, east and west are two points **seen every morning**. **The higher Moses raises the argument, the more concrete he makes the proof.**

**And the relatives of the east-west formula in the Qurʾān must be recorded:** *rabbu'l-mashāriq* (al-Ṣāffāt 37/5 — plural, "the easts"), *rabbu'l-mashriqayni wa rabbu'l-maghribayn* (al-Raḥmān 55/17 — dual). **Three verses, three different numbers: singular, dual, plural.** This difference was treated in this commentary in `037-saffat.md` and `055-rahman.md`, and the explanation recorded there was this: **the point at which the sun rises changes every day; the singular announces "direction", the dual "the two extremes", the plural "the point of each day".** I do not repeat it here.

### أَلَا تَسْتَمِعُونَ — turning to the listener

**Pharaoh gives no answer to the answer; he turns to those around him.**

**A point of word order:** the sentence stands as a question but asks for no information. **He does not suppose that they failed to hear what was said;** he knows they heard it. **The work of the sentence is to make the listener a witness:** "look at what this man is saying".
