**And the verb is worth recording: *tastamiʿūn* — to listen.** In the fifteenth verse the guarantee God gave Moses was from the same root and in the same pattern: *innā maʿakum **mustamiʿūn***. **The sūra thus uses the same verb in two different mouths:**

| Verse | Who | Expression | What it means |
|---|---|---|---|
| **15** | **God** | *Innā maʿakum mustamiʿūn* | **"We are listening"** — a guarantee |
| **25** | **Pharaoh** | *Alā tastamiʿūn* | **"Listen to this"** — mockery |

**This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.** And **as my own reading** I record this: the guarantee given at the start of the scene comes from the same root as the verb that becomes an object of mockery in the middle of the scene. **The messenger is not alone — and the sentence that tells him so uses the same word as the sentence that mocks him.** That this link is intended is not stated in the text.

### لَمَجْنُون — the accusation itself

The root **ج-ن-ن**: **to cover, to hide.** *Janna'l-layl* — the night covered over. From the same root: *jinn* (the unseen), *janna* (the garden covered by trees), *janīn* (the covered one in the womb), *junna* (a shield), *majnūn* (**one whose reason is covered over**).

**The root was analysed across the series** (`072-cin.md`, `068-kalem.md` 68/2, `052-tur.md`) **and is not repeated.**

**Two things are to be added here.**

**One — the position of the accusation.** The accusation comes **after Moses' most reasonable answer**. What he said in the twenty-sixth verse was this: "your Lord and the Lord of your forefathers". There is nothing unintelligible in it. **The accusation is given not to unintelligibility but to unacceptability.**

**Two — the word order of the accusation.** Pharaoh does not say *"you are possessed"*. **He turns to the listeners and speaks in the third person:** *inna **rasūlakum**u'lladhī ursila **ilaykum*** — "**your** messenger who was sent **to you**".

| Word | Whose the pronoun is | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| *Rasūla-kum* | **Yours** | Without denying the messengership, by saying "yours" he separates it from himself |
| *Ursila **ilaykum*** | **To you** | He does not count himself as the one addressed |

**Pharaoh thus does two things at once:** he removes Moses from being the one who addresses, and he removes himself from being the one addressed. **He says "he was sent not to me but to you".**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the two pronouns.** And the accusation *la-majnūn* at the start of the sūra is the brother of the accusation of poetry at the end of the sūra: **both classify the speaker instead of discussing what is said.** This link will be treated in the section on 224-226.

---

## 26/29-31 — قَالَ لَئِنِ ٱتَّخَذْتَ إِلَٰهًا غَيْرِى لَأَجْعَلَنَّكَ مِنَ ٱلْمَسْجُونِينَ · قَالَ أَوَلَوْ جِئْتُكَ بِشَىْءٍ مُّبِينٍ · قَالَ فَأْتِ بِهِۦٓ إِن كُنتَ مِنَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِينَ

*Qāla la-ini'ttakhadhta ilāhan ghayrī la-ajʿalannaka mina'l-masjūnīn · Qāla a-wa law jiʾtuka bi-shayʾin mubīn · Qāla fa'ti bihi in kunta mina'ṣ-ṣādiqīn*

"[Pharaoh] said: '**If you take a god other than me, I will surely make you one of the imprisoned.**' · [Moses] said: '**Even if I bring you something clear?**' · [Pharaoh] said: '**Bring it, then, if you are one of the truthful!**'"

### From proof to threat

**Pharaoh's fourth utterance ends the debate.** What ran from the twenty-third verse to the twenty-eighth was a question and answer; in the twenty-ninth verse **the response is given not to the answer but to the speaker.**

**The order is worth recording:**

| Stage | Verses | Pharaoh's move |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 23 | **Question** |
| 2 | 25 | **Turning to the listener** |
| 3 | 27 | **Classification** — "he is possessed" |
| 4 | **29** | **Threat** — "prison" |

**I record this as an observation:** four stages advancing along a line that moves away from argument. **And at the last stage Pharaoh states his own claim openly for the first time:** *ilāhan ghayrī* — "a god other than me". In the eighteenth verse he had implied it with a verb (*a-lam nurabbika*); now he says it with a word.

### مِنَ ٱلْمَسْجُونِين — a point of word order

**A point of word order worth recording, and a mould that will be repeated in the sūra.**

Pharaoh does not say *"I will imprison you"* (*la-asjunannaka*). He says **"I will make you one of the imprisoned"**: *la-ajʿalannaka **mina'l-masjūnīn***.

**In Arabic this construction (verb + *min* + definite plural) means adding a person to a group**, and the lexicographers record that it makes the threat heavier: **the person does not become a prisoner on his own; he is put inside a known class.**

**And this mould occurs more than fifteen times in the sūra.** Often enough to deserve a table:

| Verse | Expression | Who says it |
|---|---|---|
| 20 | *Wa anā mina'ḍ-ḍāllīn* | Moses |
| 21 | *Wa jaʿalanī mina'l-mursalīn* | Moses |
| **29** | *La-ajʿalannaka mina'l-masjūnīn* | **Pharaoh** |
| 31 | *In kunta mina'ṣ-ṣādiqīn* | Pharaoh |
| 42 | *Wa innakum idhan la-mina'l-muqarrabīn* | Pharaoh |
| 51 | *An kunnā awwala'l-muʾminīn* | The sorcerers |
| 116 | *La-takūnanna mina'l-marjūmīn* | The people of Noah |
| 167 | *La-takūnanna mina'l-mukhrajīn* | The people of Lot |
| 168 | *Innī li-ʿamalikum mina'l-qālīn* | Lot |
| 186 | *Wa in naẓunnuka la-mina'l-kādhibīn* | The people of al-Ayka |
| 213 | *Fa-takūna mina'l-muʿadhdhabīn* | (God, to the messenger) |

**The sūra thus recounts people not one by one but by membership of a group.** Everyone is placed inside a plural: among the messengers, among the sorcerers, among the liars, among the expelled, among those punished.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the count above:** the closing of the sūra uses the same technique — *wa'sh-shuʿarāʾu yattabiʿuhumu'l-**ghāwūn*** (224) and *illā **'lladhīna** āmanū* (227). **At its end too the sūra makes a distinction of groups.**

### أَوَلَوْ جِئْتُكَ بِشَىْءٍ مُّبِينٍ — the work of indefiniteness

**شَىْءٍ مُّبِين** — "something clear". **Indefinite.** Moses does not say what he will bring.

**A point of word order:** leaving the noun indefinite allows the claim to be set up **against the threat**. Moses is not bargaining, he promises no miracle — **he asks for the ground of the threat:** "even if I bring a proof?"

**And the *a-wa law* pattern must be recorded:** hamza (interrogative) + *wāw* + *law* (conditional). **In English: "even if…?"** This pattern does the same work in another place in the Qurʾān: *a-wa law jiʾtukum bi-ahdā mimmā wajadtum ʿalayhi ābāʾakum* (al-Zukhruf 43/24 — treated in `043-zuhruf.md`). **In both verses the pattern is set against a closed attitude.**

### فَأْتِ بِهِۦٓ — the meaning of the acceptance

**Pharaoh does not withdraw the threat; but he accepts the demand.**

**I record this as an observation:** the scene continues with permission given for the proof to be shown. **And after the proof is shown (32-33), Pharaoh's reaction will be not to reject the proof but to name it:** *inna hādhā la-sāḥirun ʿalīm* (34).

**إِن كُنتَ مِنَ ٱلصَّٰدِقِين** — the same mould again: the person is assigned to a group. And the conditional particle is *in*, not *idhā* — **in Arabic *in* is used for the doubtful, *idhā* for the certain.**

---

## 26/32-37 — فَأَلْقَىٰ عَصَاهُ فَإِذَا هِىَ ثُعْبَانٌ مُّبِينٌ · وَنَزَعَ يَدَهُۥ فَإِذَا هِىَ بَيْضَآءُ لِلنَّٰظِرِينَ · قَالَ لِلْمَلَإِ حَوْلَهُۥٓ إِنَّ هَٰذَا لَسَٰحِرٌ عَلِيمٌ · يُرِيدُ أَن يُخْرِجَكُم مِّنْ أَرْضِكُم بِسِحْرِهِۦ فَمَاذَا تَأْمُرُونَ · قَالُوٓا۟ أَرْجِهْ وَأَخَاهُ وَٱبْعَثْ فِى ٱلْمَدَآئِنِ حَٰشِرِينَ · يَأْتُوكَ بِكُلِّ سَحَّارٍ عَلِيمٍ

*Fa-alqā ʿaṣāhu fa-idhā hiya thuʿbānun mubīn · Wa nazaʿa yadahu fa-idhā hiya bayḍāʾu li'n-nāẓirīn · Qāla li'l-malaʾi ḥawlahu inna hādhā la-sāḥirun ʿalīm · Yurīdu an yukhrijakum min arḍikum bi-siḥrih; fa-mādhā taʾmurūn · Qālū arjih wa akhāhu wa'bʿath fi'l-madāʾini ḥāshirīn · Yaʾtūka bi-kulli saḥḥārin ʿalīm*

"Thereupon he cast his staff; **and there it was, a clear serpent!** · And he drew out his hand; **and there it was, white to the beholders!** · [Pharaoh] said to the chiefs around him: '**This man is certainly a knowing sorcerer. · He wants to drive you out of your land with his sorcery. What do you command?**' · They said: '**Hold him and his brother back, and send gatherers into the cities · to bring you every knowing sorcerer.**'"

### فَإِذَا هِىَ — the particle of suddenness

**إِذَا** here is not the conditional particle but what the lexicographers call ***idhā fujāʾiyya*** (the *idhā* of suddenness). **Its work is to announce unexpectedness.** In English it is rendered by "and there it was".

**And the particle is repeated in both verses:** *fa-idhā hiya thuʿbān*, *fa-idhā hiya bayḍāʾ*. **Both miracles are thus given with the same suddenness.**

**A point of word order:** the verse does not recount a transformation. It does not say "the staff turned into a snake"; **it says "he cast it — and there it was, a serpent"**. **The process in between is not in the text.** This is so in the other places where the Qurʾān recounts this scene as well (al-Aʿrāf 7/107, Ṭāhā 20/20).

### ثُعْبَان — the choice of the word

**In this scene the Qurʾān uses three different words for what the staff turned into, and all three are for the same event.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text:

| Word | Where | What it announces |
|---|---|---|
| **ثُعْبَان** (*thuʿbān*) | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/32**; al-Aʿrāf 7/107 | **A great snake** |
| **حَيَّة** (*ḥayya*) | Ṭāhā 20/20 | **A snake** (the general name) |
| **جَآنّ** (*jānn*) | Al-Naml 27/10; al-Qaṣaṣ 28/31 | **A small, swift snake** |

**The apparent tension is this: the same thing is likened both to a large and to a small snake.** The transmitted explanations: (a) the *jānn* comparison describes **the movement** (the swift coiling), the *thuʿbān* **the body**; (b) they are descriptions belonging to two different moments. **I do not impose a preference;** the first explanation accords with the dictionary sense of the words, because the distinguishing feature the lexicographers give for *jānn* is speed, not size.

**And this scene was treated at `028-kasas.md` 28/29-35;** what was recorded there was Moses' reaction: *wallā mudbiran wa lam yuʿaqqib* — he fled without looking back. **Al-Shuʿarāʾ does not recount that reaction.** Because the sūra sets the scene in Pharaoh's presence, it gives no place to Moses' fear at his first encounter.

### وَنَزَعَ يَدَهُۥ — the second verse

The root **ن-ز-ع**: **to pull out, to tear away.** The root was analysed at `079-naziat.md` 79/1 and is not repeated.

**بَيْضَآءُ لِلنَّٰظِرِين** — "white to the beholders". **A note: the whiteness is described in the eyes of those who look.** The miracle therefore stands not as a subjective experience but **before witnesses**.

**Elsewhere the Qurʾān adds a qualification to this whiteness:** *min ghayri sūʾ* — "with no disease in it" (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/32; Ṭāhā 20/22). **Al-Shuʿarāʾ does not enter this qualification.** This is a difference that can be verified from the text, and it may be explained thus: in al-Qaṣaṣ the address is to Moses (he is looking at his own hand), in al-Shuʿarāʾ the scene is in Pharaoh's presence. **I record this as my own reading.**

### إِنَّ هَٰذَا لَسَٰحِرٌ عَلِيمٌ — naming instead of denying

**Pharaoh does not say "this is a lie" or "this is a trick". He accepts the event and names it.**

**And the name he chooses is worth recording:** *sāḥirun ʿalīm* — "a **knowing** sorcerer". Not a belittlement; **a concession of expertise.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the attribute itself:** it was not possible to deny the event, because everyone saw it. **The way to neutralise an event that cannot be denied is to place it in a known category.** Sorcery was a field known in that society and having its experts. **Pharaoh therefore meets the event not with "it did not happen" but with "we know what kind of thing this is".**

**The same technique is repeated in the Qurʾān:** *hādhā siḥrun mubīn* (al-Aḥqāf 46/7 — treated in `046-ahkaf.md`), *hādhā siḥrun wa innā bihi kāfirūn* (al-Zukhruf 43/30). **And at the end of the sūra the same technique will be applied to Muhammad: "a poet", "possessed".**

### يُرِيدُ أَن يُخْرِجَكُم مِّنْ أَرْضِكُم — the shifting of the accusation

**And in the second verse the accusation changes. It is no longer sorcery but politics.**

| Verse | Accusation | To whom addressed |
|---|---|---|
| **34** | *Sāḥirun ʿalīm* | *Al-malaʾ* — the chiefs |
| **35** | *Yurīdu an yukhrijakum min arḍikum* | **The same group** |

**A point of word order:** the two sentences come one after the other and the pronoun changes. In the first, *hādhā* (this) — Moses as an object. In the second, **twice *kum*** — *yukhrija-**kum***, *arḍi-**kum***. **The threat is thus shifted onto the listener:** "you", "your land".

**And the word *arḍikum* must be recorded:** Moses was born on that soil, and Pharaoh said so with his own mouth in the eighteenth verse (*wa labithta fīnā min ʿumurika sinīn*). **Now the same soil is described as "yours" and Moses is counted an outsider.**

**I record this as an observation:** in the same conversation the same person is presented first as an insider (we raised you) and then as an outsider (he will drive you from your land). **Two descriptions serving two different purposes.**

### فَمَاذَا تَأْمُرُونَ — handing over the command

**This sentence is one of the most striking points of word order in the sūra, and it carries a judgement on its own.**

**تَأْمُرُونَ** — from the root *amr*: **to command.** Pharaoh does not say to the chiefs *"what do you think?"* (*mādhā tarawn*) or *"what is your opinion?"* (*mā raʾyukum*). **He says "what do you command?"**

**One verse earlier he had said of himself *ilāhan ghayrī* ("a god other than me") (29).** Five verses later he asks those around him "what do you command?"

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the distance between the two words within the sūra:** six verses stand between the claim to divinity and the sentence of consultation. **The text does not interpret this tension — it sets the two side by side.**

**And the same scene occurs with the same word elsewhere in the Qurʾān:** al-Aʿrāf 7/110 — *fa-mādhā taʾmurūn.* **The sentence is thus exactly the same in two sūras.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

### أَرْجِهْ — the vagueness of the word

**Two readings are transmitted about the root of the verb, and both change the meaning:**

| Root | Sense | The sentence's meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **ر-ج-و / ر-ج-أ** (*arjaʾa*) | To postpone, to delay | **"Keep him waiting"** — do not decide, stall |
| **ر-ج-و** (*irjāʾ*, deferral) | To detain | **"Hold him"** |

**Both readings converge on "do nothing immediately".** Variant readings of the word are also transmitted (*arjih*, *arjihi*, *arjiʾhu*); **these are differences of pronunciation and do not change the meaning, so I do not go into their detail.**

**And the advice given is worth recording:** the chiefs propose neither killing nor imprisonment. **What they propose is a counter-display:** to gather the experts of the same field and have the same work done.

### حَٰشِرِين and سَحَّار — two patterns

**حَٰشِرِين** — root **ح-ش-ر**: **to gather in one place what is scattered.** The root was analysed at `081-tekvir.md` 81/5 and in `050-kaf.md`. **The word is used in the context of the resurrection too (*ḥashr*), and its use here is concrete: officials going city by city to collect men.**

**سَحَّار** — root **س-ح-ر**, **the *faʿʿāl* pattern: an intensive — one who does the thing much and continually.** In the thirty-fourth verse *sāḥir* (an active participle) was used of Moses; here ***saḥḥār*** is used of the men who are to be brought.

| Verse | Word | Pattern | Of whom |
|---|---|---|---|
| **34** | *sāḥir* | Active participle | **Moses** |
| **37** | *saḥḥār* | *Faʿʿāl* — intensive | **The sorcerers to be brought** |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the difference of the two patterns:** the other side's logic of bringing "a bigger one" is reflected in the pattern of the word. **The answer is thus built on degree, not on kind.**

**And the core of the root س-ح-ر must be recorded:** the lexicographers describe the root as **"to turn a thing away from its true direction, to turn the eye or the perception".** From the same root: **سَحَر** (*saḥar*) — the end of the night, the hour of turning toward day. **Sorcery, then, changes not the reality but the perception** — and this will be confirmed in the forty-fifth verse in the text's own words: *talqafu mā **yaʾfikūn*** — "it was swallowing what they were fabricating".

---

## 26/38-42 — فَجُمِعَ ٱلسَّحَرَةُ لِمِيقَٰتِ يَوْمٍ مَّعْلُومٍ · وَقِيلَ لِلنَّاسِ هَلْ أَنتُم مُّجْتَمِعُونَ · لَعَلَّنَا نَتَّبِعُ ٱلسَّحَرَةَ إِن كَانُوا۟ هُمُ ٱلْغَٰلِبِينَ · فَلَمَّا جَآءَ ٱلسَّحَرَةُ قَالُوا۟ لِفِرْعَوْنَ أَئِنَّ لَنَا لَأَجْرًا إِن كُنَّا نَحْنُ ٱلْغَٰلِبِينَ · قَالَ نَعَمْ وَإِنَّكُمْ إِذًا لَّمِنَ ٱلْمُقَرَّبِينَ

*Fa-jumiʿa's-saḥaratu li-mīqāti yawmin maʿlūm · Wa qīla li'n-nāsi hal antum mujtamiʿūn · Laʿallanā nattabiʿu's-saḥarata in kānū humu'l-ghālibīn · Fa-lammā jāʾa's-saḥaratu qālū li-Firʿawna a-inna lanā la-ajran in kunnā naḥnu'l-ghālibīn · Qāla naʿam wa innakum idhan la-mina'l-muqarrabīn*

"**And so the sorcerers were gathered for the appointed hour of a known day.** · And it was said to the people: '**Will you gather too? · Perhaps we shall follow the sorcerers — if they are the ones who prevail.**' · When the sorcerers came they said to Pharaoh: '**Will there really be a wage for us if we are the ones who prevail?**' · '**Yes**', he said, '**and then you will certainly be among those brought near.**'"

### فَجُمِعَ — the passive voice

**In the first three of the five verses the verbs are passive: *jumiʿa* (were gathered), *qīla* (it was said).** The agent is not named.

**A point of word order:** the passive voice, as it hides the one who acts, also **announces the breadth of the act**. Gatherers had been sent city by city (36), the sorcerers had been brought, the people had been summoned. **All of this is fitted into a single verb.**

**And an observation: the text recounts the mobilisation of a state with two verbs.**

### لِمِيقَٰتِ يَوْمٍ مَّعْلُوم — the fixing of the hour

**مِيقَٰت** — root **و-ق-ت**: an appointed time. *Mīqāt* is not "a time" but **"a meeting whose time is fixed"**; the word has this sense in the terms of the pilgrimage too.

**And *yawmin maʿlūm* — "a known day".** The Qurʾān does not say what day this event was. **At Ṭāhā 20/59 Moses' own proposal occurs:** *mawʿidukum yawmu'z-zīnati wa an yuḥshara'n-nāsu ḍuḥā* — "your appointment is the day of adornment (the festival); let the people be gathered in the forenoon". **Al-Shuʿarāʾ does not give this detail; Ṭāhā does.** The two texts do not conflict; one is shorter than the other.

**I do not enter into detail the Qurʾān does not give;** reports are transmitted about which festival it was and how many sorcerers came, but they are not in the Qurʾān.

### هَلْ أَنتُم مُّجْتَمِعُونَ — the summoning of the people

**The sentence stands as a question but its work is a summons.** In Arabic the interrogative pattern can be used for urging. The English "will you gather?" carries the same doubleness.

**And what must be recorded is this: the display is open to the public.** It is held not in a closed hall but **before a crowd**. **This is what will determine the outcome of the scene:** when the sorcerers fall prostrate in the forty-sixth verse, the whole people will see it.

### لَعَلَّنَا نَتَّبِعُ ٱلسَّحَرَةَ — the sentence placed in the people's mouth

**A point of word order worth recording, and one that has been disputed: who says this sentence?**

| Reading | Who says it | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **Those making the summons** | The officials / Pharaoh's side | It is the continuation of the same sentence as *qīla li'n-nās* |
| **The people themselves** | The gathered crowd | The verb is first person plural (*nattabiʿu*) |

**Both readings can be supported from the text. I do not impose a preference.**

**But the content of the sentence, whichever reading is taken, says the same thing, and it must be recorded:** *in kānū humu'l-**ghālibīn*** — "if **they** are the ones who **prevail**". **The measure is not truth but victory.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the word:** the sentence does not say "if they turn out to be in the right" or "if they speak the truth". **Whoever wins the contest is to be followed.** And ten verses after setting up this sentence, the sūra will recount that the winners of the contest **themselves changed sides** (46-48). **The measure thus breaks itself.**

The root **غ-ل-ب**: **to prevail, to overcome.** The word occurs three times in the sūra (40, 41, 44), and all three times it belongs to the side of the sorcerers: twice the expectation of the people or the sorcerers, once their own oath. **There is no fourth occurrence — because they do not prevail.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

### أَئِنَّ لَنَا لَأَجْرًا — the question of the wage

**And one of the strongest oppositions in the sūra is here.**

**The first question the sorcerers put to Pharaoh is about a wage:** *a-inna lanā la-ajrā.*

**And in the sūra five prophets will say the same sentence five times:** *wa mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min **ajrin*** (109, 127, 145, 164, 180) — "**I ask you no wage for it**".

| | **The sorcerers 26/41** | **The five prophets (109, 127, 145, 164, 180)** |
|---|---|---|
| **Word** | *ajr* | *ajr* |
| **Verb** | *A-inna lanā* — "is there for us?" | *Mā asʾalukum* — "I do not ask" |
| **From whom** | **From Pharaoh** | **From no one** |
| **Its return** | *Mina'l-muqarrabīn* — to be brought near | *ʿAlā rabbi'l-ʿālamīn* — it falls upon the Lord |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is that the same word (*ajr*) occurs six times in the sūra:** once asked for, five times refused. **And the sūra will recount that those who asked for the wage in the end gave the wage up** (50-51). **The word *ajr* is thus a separator in the sūra:** it measures who is speaking in return for what.

### ٱلْمُقَرَّبِين — the promise of nearness

The root **ق-ر-ب**: **to be near.** *Muqarrab* — the passive participle of Form II: **brought near, taken into proximity.**

**The word is used in two different fields in the Qurʾān, and the difference must be recorded:**

| Use | Example | Nearness to whom |
|---|---|---|
| **The language of the court** | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/42** — Pharaoh's promise | **To the ruler** |
| **The language of the hereafter** | Al-Wāqiʿa 56/11 (*ulāʾika'l-muqarrabūn*), al-Muṭaffifīn 83/21 | **To God** |

**This word was analysed in `056-vakia.md` and in `083-mutaffifin.md`, and I do not repeat it.**

**What is to be added here is an observation:** Pharaoh answers the question about the wage with "yes" and adds something on top of it — **nearness.** What is offered, then, is not money but **position**. And a few verses later the sorcerers will refuse that position: *lā ḍayra innā ilā rabbinā munqalibūn* (50). **What was set before them was a position; what they left behind was a position too.**

---

## 26/43-49 — قَالَ لَهُم مُّوسَىٰٓ أَلْقُوا۟ مَآ أَنتُم مُّلْقُونَ · فَأَلْقَوْا۟ حِبَالَهُمْ وَعِصِيَّهُمْ وَقَالُوا۟ بِعِزَّةِ فِرْعَوْنَ إِنَّا لَنَحْنُ ٱلْغَٰلِبُونَ · فَأَلْقَىٰ مُوسَىٰ عَصَاهُ فَإِذَا هِىَ تَلْقَفُ مَا يَأْفِكُونَ · فَأُلْقِىَ ٱلسَّحَرَةُ سَٰجِدِينَ · قَالُوٓا۟ ءَامَنَّا بِرَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · رَبِّ مُوسَىٰ وَهَٰرُونَ · قَالَ ءَامَنتُمْ لَهُۥ قَبْلَ أَنْ ءَاذَنَ لَكُمْ … لَأُقَطِّعَنَّ أَيْدِيَكُمْ وَأَرْجُلَكُم مِّنْ خِلَٰفٍ وَلَأُصَلِّبَنَّكُمْ أَجْمَعِينَ

*Qāla lahum Mūsā alqū mā antum mulqūn · Fa-alqaw ḥibālahum wa ʿiṣiyyahum wa qālū bi-ʿizzati Firʿawna innā la-naḥnu'l-ghālibūn · Fa-alqā Mūsā ʿaṣāhu fa-idhā hiya talqafu mā yaʾfikūn · Fa-ulqiya's-saḥaratu sājidīn · Qālū āmannā bi-rabbi'l-ʿālamīn · Rabbi Mūsā wa Hārūn · Qāla āmantum lahu qabla an ādhana lakum… la-uqaṭṭiʿanna aydiyakum wa arjulakum min khilāfin wa la-uṣallibannakum ajmaʿīn*

"Moses said to them: '**Cast what you are going to cast!**' · **So they cast their ropes and their staffs and said: 'By the might of Pharaoh, we are surely the ones who prevail!'** · Then Moses cast his staff; **and there it was, swallowing what they had fabricated!** · Thereupon **the sorcerers were thrown down prostrate.** · They said: '**We believe in the Lord of all the worlds — the Lord of Moses and Aaron.**' · [Pharaoh] said: '**You believed him before I gave you leave!… I will surely cut off your hands and your feet on opposite sides, and I will surely crucify you all!**'"

### ل-ق-ي / ل-ق-ف — nine castings in seven verses

**The clearest structural datum of this section is the repetition of a single root. The root ل-ق-ي (to cast) occurs six times in seven verses, and its relative ل-ق-ف once:**

| Verse | Word | Who casts | Mood |
|---|---|---|---|
| 43 | *alqū* | The sorcerers | **Imperative** — from Moses' mouth |
| 43 | *mulqūn* | The sorcerers | Active participle |
| 44 | *fa-alqaw* | The sorcerers | Perfect |
| 45 | *fa-alqā* | **Moses** | Perfect |
| 45 | *talqafu* | **The staff** | Imperfect — **ل-ق-ف** |
| 46 | *fa-ulqiya* | **The sorcerers** | **Passive** |

**And the last row must be recorded: at the sixth occurrence the voice changes.**

For four verses the sorcerers were the side that **casts**; in the forty-sixth verse they become the side that **is cast**: *fa-**ulqiya**'s-saḥaratu sājidīn* — "the sorcerers **were thrown down** prostrate".

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the change of voice:** *fa-sajada's-saḥaratu* ("they prostrated themselves") could have been said; it was not. **The passive voice announces that the prostration was not a planned decision but an irresistible reaction** — as though something set them down. The same mould occurs at al-Aʿrāf 7/120 as well (*wa ulqiya's-saḥaratu sājidīn*).

**تَلْقَفُ** — root **ل-ق-ف**: **to snatch a thing up quickly and swallow it.** *Laqifa* — to catch something in the air. **The word says not "it destroyed" but "it swallowed":** it took it in as a body.

### بِعِزَّةِ فِرْعَوْنَ — the oath

**The sorcerers swear an oath, and what they swear by must be recorded: *ʿizzatu Firʿawn* — the might of Pharaoh.**

The root **ع-ز-ز** is the root of the name *al-ʿAzīz* in the seal of the sūra (9, 68, 104, 122, 140, 159, 175, 191), and it was analysed in `035-fatir.md`. **Its core: hardness, resistance to wear, invincibility.**

**The sūra thus uses the same word eight times of God and once of Pharaoh.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

**And this is another instance of the technique recorded in `085-buruc.md`:** the Qurʾān takes the attribute the other side has assigned to itself and returns it to its true owner. **The sorcerers swear by the *ʿizza* of Pharaoh; at the end of every account the sūra stamps its seal, "the true *ʿazīz* is your Lord".** I record this link as a reading of the Qurʾān by the Qurʾān.

**A point of word order:** the oath is sworn **before** the miracle takes place, and it carries three elements announcing certainty: *innā* + *la-* + *naḥnu* (the separating pronoun) + *al-ghālibūn* (definite). **In Arabic this is one of the highest degrees of certainty.** In English: "we, we most certainly are the ones who prevail."

**And four verses later the same mouths will say *āmannā bi-rabbi'l-ʿālamīn*.**

### مَا يَأْفِكُونَ — the choice of the word

The root **أ-ف-ك**: **to turn a thing from its direction, to overturn it.** *Ifk* — a turned-about speech, a slander. The root was treated at `045-casiye.md` 45/7 and at `046-ahkaf.md` 46/11, 46/28.

**And this word will occur twice in the sūra:**

| Verse | Word | Of whom |
|---|---|---|
| **45** | *mā **yaʾfikūn*** | **The sorcerers** — what they fabricate |
| **222** | ***affākin*** athīm | **The one the satans come down upon** |

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the shared root:** the sūra names the sorcerer's work and the *affāk*'s work with the same root — **both turn a thing away from its true direction.** And at the end of the sūra it will be said that the source of the Qurʾān does not come from this field (210-212). **The sūra thus builds its own defence here, between a staff and some ropes.**

**And a point of word order:** the verse does not say *"it swallowed their ropes"*. *Talqafu **mā yaʾfikūn*** — "it was swallowing **what they were fabricating**". **What is swallowed is not an object but an act.**

### ءَامَنَّا بِرَبِّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ · رَبِّ مُوسَىٰ وَهَٰرُونَ — two verses, one correction

**The forty-seventh verse is a declaration of faith; the forty-eighth adds an explanation to it: *rabbi Mūsā wa Hārūn*.**

**Why this was added has been disputed, and there is an explanation from within the text. I record it as my own reading:** the expression *rabbu'l-ʿālamīn* is one that Pharaoh **could appropriate to himself** — indeed Pharaoh had said of himself *ilāhan ghayrī* (29), and in another sūra the Qurʾān records him saying *anā rabbukumu'l-aʿlā* (al-Nāziʿāt 79/24 — treated in `079-naziat.md`). **The addition in the forty-eighth verse closes off the possibility:** which Lord is meant is specified by name.

**This explanation rests on a tension within the text itself and is not necessary.**

### ءَامَنتُمْ لَهُۥ قَبْلَ أَنْ ءَاذَنَ لَكُمْ — the place of leave

**There are two sentences in Pharaoh's reaction, and both bear on the whole of the sūra.**

**One — *qabla an ādhana lakum* ("before I gave you leave").** Faith is spoken of as a matter subject to permission.

**I record this as an observation:** the sentence shows what the claim to divinity amounts to. **The claim includes not being worshipped but the authority to decide who will believe in what.**

**And here *āmantum lahu* occurs — *lahu*, not *bihi*.** **In Arabic *āmana bi-* is "to believe in a thing", *āmana li-* is "to trust someone, to hold him true".** Pharaoh therefore does not say "you have believed in God"; he says **"you have trusted him"** — again pulling the subject onto the person.

**Two — *innahu la-kabīrukumu'lladhī ʿallamakumu's-siḥr* ("he is your chief, who taught you sorcery").** The event, in other words, is explained as a conspiracy: master and pupils had come to an understanding.

**This is the continuation of the naming in the thirty-fourth verse.** An event that cannot be denied is explained by a known cause. **And there is a problem inside the explanation, and the text makes no attempt to repair it:** the sorcerers had only just been gathered from the cities (36-38), and Moses had not been in Egypt for years (21). **The text does not point out this contradiction; it sets it before the reader.** I record this as an observation.

### مِّنْ خِلَٰفٍ — the description of the punishment

The root **خ-ل-ف**: **the back, the opposite, what takes another's place.** *Min khilāf* — **on opposite sides**: the right hand with the left foot, or the reverse.

**The punishment is described in detail in the text: cutting off and crucifixion.** The Qurʾān records the same threat in the same words at al-Aʿrāf 7/124 and Ṭāhā 20/71 as well.

**I enter a note, and it follows from the principle of this commentary:** the verse establishes no penal ruling; **it transmits the words of a tyrant.** The difference between transmission and ruling is plain here — the sentence is in Pharaoh's mouth, and the sūra does not endorse it. I derive no legal ruling from it.

---

## 26/50-51 — قَالُوا۟ لَا ضَيْرَ ۖ إِنَّآ إِلَىٰ رَبِّنَا مُنقَلِبُونَ · إِنَّا نَطْمَعُ أَن يَغْفِرَ لَنَا رَبُّنَا خَطَٰيَٰنَآ أَن كُنَّآ أَوَّلَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِينَ

*Qālū lā ḍayra; innā ilā rabbinā munqalibūn · Innā naṭmaʿu an yaghfira lanā rabbunā khaṭāyānā an kunnā awwala'l-muʾminīn*

"They said: '**No harm! We are returning to our Lord. · We hope that our Lord will forgive us our faults, because we are the first of the believers.**'"

### The greatness of the answer

**The threat contained three things: cutting off, crucifixion, all of them.** The answer begins with two words: ***lā ḍayra*** — "no harm".

**This is the shortest and the most loaded answer in the sūra. And its structure must be recorded:**

| Element | Expression | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Lā ḍayra* | **The refusal itself** — the threat is not weighed |
| 2 | *Innā ilā rabbinā munqalibūn* | **The ground** — the place of arrival |
| 3 | *Innā naṭmaʿu an yaghfira lanā* | **Hope** — the reckoning of the past |

**The answer is thus built in three steps: refusal, ground, hope.** And the ground takes up not the **content** of the threat but its **outcome**.

### لَا ضَيْرَ — root ض-ي-ر

The root **ض-ي-ر**: **to do harm.** *Ḍayr* — harm, loss. Its relatives are *ḍarar* (ض-ر-ر) and *sūʾ*.

**The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

**And the kind of *lā* must be recorded: *lā al-nāfiya li'l-jins*** — the *lā* that negates the whole class. In Arabic this pattern means "there is no harm whatever"; it does not say "there is a little harm" or "this harm is unimportant". **The concept of harm itself is lifted away.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the pattern:** the answer does not say "we will bear the pain". **It says "this is not a harm"** — that is, the measure the threat rests on is rejected. Cutting off and crucifixion are a harm only if the body is the final value.

### مُنقَلِبُون — root ق-ل-ب

**The root was analysed in `084-insikak.md` and I do not repeat it.** The description there: **ق-ل-ب** — to turn a thing over, to bring its underside up; from the same root **قَلْب** (*qalb*, the heart) — the thing that turns most from state to state. **إِنقَلَبَ** (Form VII, *infiʿāl*) — it turned, it turned back.

**Three things are to be added here.**

**One — the word will return in the last verse of the sūra.** Verse two hundred and twenty-seven: *wa sa-yaʿlamu'lladhīna ẓalamū ayya **munqalabin yanqalibūn*** — "those who did wrong will soon know **to what place of return they will return**".

| Verse | Expression | Of whom | Tone |
|---|---|---|---|
| **50** | *Innā ilā rabbinā **munqalibūn*** | **The sorcerers** — of themselves | **Confidence** |
| **227** | *Ayya **munqalabin yanqalibūn*** | **Those who do wrong** — third person | **Warning** |

**The sūra thus uses the same root at its two ends: one speaks knowing the place of return, the other does not know it.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

**Two — the use of the active participle.** *Munqalibūn* — not "those who will return" but "those who are returning". **In Arabic the active participle can announce a state already realised or already settled.** The return is therefore recounted not as an event to happen in the future but as **the present position**.

**Three — its relative in the Qurʾān.** *Wa innā ilā rabbinā la-munqalibūn* (al-Zukhruf 43/14) — **the prayer said when mounted on a beast.** That passage was treated at `043-zuhruf.md` 43/13-14. **The same sentence, in two sūras in two different scenes:** one setting out on a journey, the other under threat of execution. **This is an overlap that can be verified from the text, and it shows what the word announces: every movement has a point of arrival.**

### أَن كُنَّآ أَوَّلَ ٱلْمُؤْمِنِين — "the first of the believers"

**The expression has been disputed: the first of what?**

| Reading | Its meaning | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **The first of that assembly** | The first to believe among those present | The scene is an assembly for a display; the people had gathered (39) |
| **The first of Egypt** | The first to believe among the people of Pharaoh | The verse sets no limit |
| **The first of those outside Moses' people** | The Children of Israel had already believed | It is not stated openly in the text |

**I do not impose a preference.** What is to be recorded is this: **men who a few minutes earlier were asking Pharaoh for a wage now describe themselves as "the first of the believers"** — and they say it not as a boast but as **the ground of a hope of forgiveness**.

### نَطْمَعُ — the word for hope

The root **ط-م-ع**: **to want something intensely, to have one's eye on it.** In Arabic the word often carries a negative colouring (covetousness); **here it is used for a hope that is positive.**

**And the same word will occur once more in the sūra — in Abraham's mouth:** *wa'lladhī **aṭmaʿu** an yaghfira lī khaṭīʾatī yawma'd-dīn* (82).

| Verse | Who | Expression |
|---|---|---|
| **51** | **The sorcerers** | *Innā **naṭmaʿu** an yaghfira lanā rabbunā **khaṭāyānā*** |
| **82** | **Abraham** | *Wa'lladhī **aṭmaʿu** an yaghfira lī **khaṭīʾatī*** |

**The two sentences are almost exactly the same: the same verb (*ṭamaʿ*), the same request (*an yaghfira*), the same root (خ-ط-ء).** There are two differences: the sorcerers are **plural** (*naṭmaʿu*, *lanā*, *khaṭāyānā*), Abraham **singular** (*aṭmaʿu*, *lī*, *khaṭīʾatī*).

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the overlap of the two sentences:** the sūra builds the prayer of a prophet and the prayer of men who were sorcerers an hour before **out of the same words**. **It observes no difference of rank between them.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text; what it means is interpretation.

### What this says to the present

What the scene records is not a change of opinion. **Men who came for a wage give up, within a few minutes, the wage, the position and life itself.** And in doing so their being experts in their own trade plays its part: **they knew better than anyone in that assembly what the thing they saw was not.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the thirty-seventh verse:** those brought were *saḥḥārin **ʿalīm***, that is, "knowing" sorcerers. **Here knowledge becomes the cause not of resistance but of surrender.**

---

## 26/52-59 — وَأَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَىٰ مُوسَىٰٓ أَنْ أَسْرِ بِعِبَادِىٓ إِنَّكُم مُّتَّبَعُونَ · فَأَرْسَلَ فِرْعَوْنُ فِى ٱلْمَدَآئِنِ حَٰشِرِينَ · إِنَّ هَٰٓؤُلَآءِ لَشِرْذِمَةٌ قَلِيلُونَ · وَإِنَّهُمْ لَنَا لَغَآئِظُونَ · وَإِنَّا لَجَمِيعٌ حَٰذِرُونَ · فَأَخْرَجْنَٰهُم مِّن جَنَّٰتٍ وَعُيُونٍ · وَكُنُوزٍ وَمَقَامٍ كَرِيمٍ · كَذَٰلِكَ ۖ وَأَوْرَثْنَٰهَا بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ

*Wa awḥaynā ilā Mūsā an asri bi-ʿibādī innakum muttabaʿūn · Fa-arsala Firʿawnu fi'l-madāʾini ḥāshirīn · Inna hāʾulāʾi la-shirdhimatun qalīlūn · Wa innahum lanā la-ghāʾiẓūn · Wa innā la-jamīʿun ḥādhirūn · Fa-akhrajnāhum min jannātin wa ʿuyūn · Wa kunūzin wa maqāmin karīm · Kadhālika wa awrathnāhā banī Isrāʾīl*

"**And We revealed to Moses: 'Set out by night with My servants; you will be pursued.'** · Pharaoh sent gatherers into the cities: · '**These are a paltry little band; · and they are enraging us; · and we are a company on our guard.**' · **So We brought them out of gardens and springs, · out of treasures and a noble station.** · So it was; **and We made the Children of Israel inherit them.**"

### أَسْرِ بِعِبَادِى — the march by night

The root **س-ر-ي**: **to travel by night.** *Isrāʾ* — a journey by night (the *asrā* of al-Isrāʾ 17/1 is from the same root). **The word contains no daytime travel; the time is inside the root.**

**And a point of word order: *bi-ʿibādī* — "with My servants".** The Children of Israel are named here not as "your people" or "them" but as **"My servants"**.

**I record this as an observation, and it ties to the whole of the sūra:** in the twenty-second verse Moses had said to Pharaoh *"you **made slaves** of the Children of Israel"* (*ʿabbadta*). **Now the same root occurs for the same community but with a different attribution: *ʿibādī* — My servants.**

| Verse | Word | Root | Attributed to |
|---|---|---|---|
| **22** | *ʿabbadta* | ع-ب-د | **To Pharaoh** — by force |
| **52** | *ʿibādī* | ع-ب-د | **To God** |

**The same root, two attributions.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text. **And as my own reading I record this:** the sūra speaks not of the abolition of servitude but of **the changing of its direction**. Deliverance is recounted not as being bound to no one but as **the correction of whom one is bound to**. This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### إِنَّكُم مُّتَّبَعُونَ — the pursuit announced in advance

**A point of word order:** the pursuit is announced before it happens, and **in the passive**: *muttabaʿūn* — "you are the pursued".

**The command to depart therefore does not hide the danger.** This resembles the mould recorded at `028-kasas.md` 28/7-13 in the announcement given to the mother: **the command is not left bare; what will happen is said.**

### شِرْذِمَة — the rarity of the word

The root **ش-ر-ذ-م**: the sense the lexicographers give is **a small piece broken off a whole, a paltry group.** *Shirdhima* — a group few in number and scattered.

**The word occurs only here in the Qurʾān.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

**And a point of word order must be recorded: *shirdhimatun qalīlūn*.** *Shirdhima* already means "a paltry group"; on top of it *qalīlūn* ("few") is added. **The fewness is thus said twice.**

**A grammatical note:** *shirdhima* is feminine singular, while its adjective is a sound masculine plural (*qalīlūn*). **This is an ordinary usage with collective nouns** — the adjective looks to the individuals of the group.

**And the work of the sentence must be recorded:** Pharaoh describes as **small** the community after which he is sending an army. **If they are small, why is an army needed?** The text does not ask this question; **it sets the sentences side by side**, and the next verse shows the contradiction of itself: *innā la-jamīʿun ḥādhirūn* — "we are a company on our guard".

### A speech of three sentences — a series of contradictions

| Verse | Expression | What it announces |
|---|---|---|
| **54** | *La-shirdhimatun qalīlūn* | **They are few** |
| **55** | *Wa innahum lanā la-ghāʾiẓūn* | **They are enraging us** |
| **56** | *Wa innā la-jamīʿun ḥādhirūn* | **We are on our guard** |

**All three sentences are reinforced with *inna* + *lām*.** All three, that is, are set down as firm claims.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the sequence of the three sentences:** the first belittles the other side, the third calls its own side to be on guard. **Contempt and precaution cannot be spoken in the same breath** — and the text shows this without criticism, merely by setting them in order.

**غَآئِظُون** — root **غ-ي-ظ**: **anger that seethes within.** The lexicographers describe *ghayẓ* as anger that is not let out, that gathers in the breast; in this it is distinguished from *ghaḍab*.

**حَٰذِرُون** — root **ح-ذ-ر**: **to be on one's guard, to take care.** Two variant readings are transmitted for the word: *ḥādhirūn* (active participle — on guard) and *ḥadhirūn* (assimilate adjective, *ṣifa mushabbaha* — continually on guard, wary). **There is a difference of sense, but a small one: the one announces being on guard at that moment, the other a wariness that has become a trait.** Both are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.

### فَأَخْرَجْنَٰهُم مِّن جَنَّٰتٍ وَعُيُونٍ — the side that is driven out

**And one of the sharpest reversals in the sūra is here.**

**In the thirty-fifth verse Pharaoh had said:** *yurīdu an **yukhrijakum** min **arḍikum*** — "he wants to **drive you out of your land**".

**What happens in the fifty-seventh verse is this:** *fa-**akhrajnāhum** min jannātin wa ʿuyūn* — "**We brought them out** of gardens and springs".

| | **26/35 — Pharaoh's fear** | **26/57 — What happened** |
|---|---|---|
| **Verb** | *Yukhrija* (Form IV) | *Akhrajnā* (Form IV) |
| **Agent** | **Moses** (as claimed) | **God** |
| **Object** | *kum* — you | *hum* — them |
| **From where** | *Min arḍikum* — from your land | *Min jannātin wa ʿuyūn* — from gardens and springs |

**The same root, the same form, the reverse direction.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

**And at `044-duhan.md` 44/25-28 the same event is recounted in the same words** (*kam tarakū min jannātin wa ʿuyūn*) **and was treated there.** The difference here is the verb: al-Dukhān says "**they left behind**", al-Shuʿarāʾ says "**We brought out**". **One recounts an abandoning, the other a driving out.**

**Four things are enumerated, and their order is worth recording:**

| Order | Word | What |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Jannāt* | **Gardens** — production |
| 2 | *ʿUyūn* | **Springs** — water |
| 3 | *Kunūz* | **Treasures** — accumulated wealth |
| 4 | *Maqāmin karīm* | **A noble station** — standing |

**Three are material; the fourth is not.** *Maqāmin karīm* is not a possession but a **position**.

**And the attribute *karīm* was used of plants in the seventh verse of the sūra** (*min kulli zawjin karīm*). **The sūra thus gives the same attribute both to what the earth gives and to what man loses.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.

### وَأَوْرَثْنَٰهَا بَنِىٓ إِسْرَٰٓءِيلَ — the transfer of the inheritance

The root **و-ر-ث**: **a thing's passing to another after its owner.**

**And this is the fulfilment of the promise treated at `028-kasas.md` 28/1-6.** The sentence recorded there was this: *wa najʿalahumu'l-**wārithīn*** (al-Qaṣaṣ 28/5) — "and make them **the inheritors**". **The same root, the same community, the same promise.**

| Sūra | Expression | Mood |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Qaṣaṣ 28/5** | *Wa najʿalahumu'l-wārithīn* | **Imperfect** — it announces a will |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/59** | *Wa awrathnāhā banī Isrāʾīl* | **Perfect** — realised |

**This is an overlap that can be verified from the text, and it is the counterpart of the promise of "the reversal of position" recorded in `028-kasas.md`.**

**كَذَٰلِكَ** — "so it was". A one-word fragment of a verse; the lexicographers explain it as **a mark that seals the preceding scene**.

---

## 26/60-68 — فَأَتْبَعُوهُم مُّشْرِقِينَ · فَلَمَّا تَرَٰٓءَا ٱلْجَمْعَانِ قَالَ أَصْحَٰبُ مُوسَىٰٓ إِنَّا لَمُدْرَكُونَ · قَالَ كَلَّآ ۖ إِنَّ مَعِىَ رَبِّى سَيَهْدِينِ · فَأَوْحَيْنَآ إِلَىٰ مُوسَىٰٓ أَنِ ٱضْرِب بِّعَصَاكَ ٱلْبَحْرَ ۖ فَٱنفَلَقَ فَكَانَ كُلُّ فِرْقٍ كَٱلطَّوْدِ ٱلْعَظِيمِ · وَأَزْلَفْنَا ثَمَّ ٱلْءَاخَرِينَ · وَأَنجَيْنَا مُوسَىٰ وَمَن مَّعَهُۥٓ أَجْمَعِينَ · ثُمَّ أَغْرَقْنَا ٱلْءَاخَرِينَ · إِنَّ فِى ذَٰلِكَ لَءَايَةً ۖ وَمَا كَانَ أَكْثَرُهُم مُّؤْمِنِينَ · وَإِنَّ رَبَّكَ لَهُوَ ٱلْعَزِيزُ ٱلرَّحِيمُ

*Fa-atbaʿūhum mushriqīn · Fa-lammā tarāʾa'l-jamʿāni qāla aṣḥābu Mūsā innā la-mudrakūn · Qāla kallā; inna maʿiya rabbī sa-yahdīn · Fa-awḥaynā ilā Mūsā ani'ḍrib bi-ʿaṣāka'l-baḥr; fa'nfalaqa fa-kāna kullu firqin ka'ṭ-ṭawdi'l-ʿaẓīm · Wa azlafnā thamma'l-ākharīn · Wa anjaynā Mūsā wa man maʿahu ajmaʿīn · Thumma aghraqna'l-ākharīn · Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan wa mā kāna aktharuhum muʾminīn · Wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*

"**They pursued them as the sun rose.** · **When the two hosts came in sight of one another, the men of Moses said: 'We are overtaken!'** · '**No!**' he said, '**my Lord is with me; He will guide me.**' · **Thereupon We revealed to Moses: 'Strike the sea with your staff.' It parted, and every part was like a great mountain.** · **And We brought the others near to that place.** · **And We saved Moses and all who were with him; · then We drowned the others.** · In this there is surely a sign; but most of them were not believers. · And your Lord — He indeed — is the Mighty, the Merciful."

### مُّشْرِقِين — the word for the hour

The root **ش-ر-ق**: the rising of the sun. *Mushriqīn* — an active participle announcing a circumstance: **"in the state of having entered upon sunrise"**, that is, as day broke.

**And a point of word order: in the fifty-second verse the departure had been by the command *asri* (travel by night).** Now the pursuit is at the hour of *mushriqīn* (as the sun rises). **The text thus separates the movement of the two sides by time:** one set out at night, the other went after them in the morning.

**A caution:** the word is *mushriqīn* (ش-ر-ق, to rise) and not *mushrikīn* (ش-ر-ك, to associate partners). **The two words are easily confused in transliteration; their roots are different.**

### تَرَٰٓءَا ٱلْجَمْعَانِ — seeing one another

**تَرَآءَىٰ** — Form VI (*tafāʿala*): **reciprocity.** *Tarāʾa'l-jamʿān* — "the two hosts saw one another". **Not a one-sided seeing.**

**ٱلْجَمْعَان** — dual: two hosts. **And the word is from the same root as the word Pharaoh used of his own side in the fifty-sixth verse:** *innā la-**jamīʿun** ḥādhirūn.*

### إِنَّا لَمُدْرَكُون — the expression of the fear

The root **د-ر-ك**: **to catch up with, to reach, to overtake.** *Mudrak* — passive participle: **overtaken, caught.**

**A point of word order:** the sentence is reinforced with *inna* + *lām* — the fear, that is, is put not as a possibility but as **a certainty**: "we are overtaken."

**And the side that speaks must be recorded: *aṣḥābu Mūsā* — "the men of Moses".** Here the sūra does not say "his people" or "the Children of Israel". **In the fifty-second verse the same community had been named *ʿibādī* ("My servants").** Three different names, three different contexts: **servants (in relation to God), men (in relation to Moses), the Children of Israel (by lineage).**

### كَلَّآ ۖ إِنَّ مَعِىَ رَبِّى سَيَهْدِينِ — the second *kallā* of the sūra

**The word occurs twice in the sūra, and both times it is said against a fear:**

| Verse | Who says it | To whom | What it rejects |
|---|---|---|---|
| **15** | **God** | To Moses | *An yaqtulūn* — the fear of being killed |
| **62** | **Moses** | To his people | *Innā la-mudrakūn* — the fear of being overtaken |

**Moses, that is, says to his people the word that was said to him.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

**And the continuation of the sentence must be recorded: *inna maʿiya rabbī*.** In the fifteenth verse the guarantee was *innā **maʿakum** mustamiʿūn* ("we are with you"). **Now Moses repeats the same word with a singular pronoun: "my Lord is with me."**

**سَيَهْدِين** — *sīn* + imperfect: **the near future.** And the verb is **هدى** — to guide.

**A point of word order:** Moses does not say "He will save us" or "He will part the sea". **He says "He will guide me".** He does not know the solution; **he knows that he will be guided.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the choice of the verb:** the revelation comes in the next verse (63). **At the moment he framed the sentence, then, Moses did not yet know the solution.** The text does not say this openly; the sequence shows it.

**And the same verb will occur once more in the sūra — in Abraham's mouth:** *alladhī khalaqanī fa-huwa **yahdīn*** (78). **Two prophets use the same verb in the same pattern.** This link will be treated in the section on 78-82.

### فَٱنفَلَقَ — the word for the parting

The root **ف-ل-ق**: **to split a thing in two, to cleave.** From the same root: *falaq* (the cleaving of the dawn — analysed in `113-felak.md`) and *fāliqu'l-ḥabbi wa'n-nawā* (al-Anʿām 6/95 — the splitter of the grain).

**Form VII (*infaʿala*) must be recorded:** *infalaqa* — **it parted of itself.** In Arabic this form announces **the result** without bringing the agent of the verb forward.

**The verse therefore does not say "God parted the sea"; the command "strike with your staff" is given, and it is said "it parted".** No agent is named between the command and the result.

### كَٱلطَّوْدِ ٱلْعَظِيم — the comparison

The root **ط-و-د**: **a high and unshakeable mountain.** *Ṭawd*, unlike an ordinary mountain (*jabal*), announces **height and immovability**.

**فِرْق** — root **ف-ر-ق**: a separated part. **That is, each part of the water.**

**The work of the comparison must be recorded:** the verse says the water divided in two and likens the divided parts to **a mountain**. **There are two points to the respect in which the comparison is made: height and stillness.** The water comes into a state of not flowing and standing high — that is, **the reverse of its own nature**.

**I record this as an observation; I set up no explanation in physics of how the water stood.** No hunting for scientific miracles is done in this commentary (`STYLE.md`).

### وَأَزْلَفْنَا ثَمَّ ٱلْءَاخَرِين — bringing near

The root **ز-ل-ف**: **to bring near, to draw close.** The same root will occur once more in the sūra: *wa **uzlifati**'l-jannatu li'l-muttaqīn* (90) — "the garden was brought near to those who guard themselves".

| Verse | Expression | Brought near to whom | What |
|---|---|---|---|
| **64** | *Wa **azlafnā** thamma'l-ākharīn* | **The others** | **The sea / destruction** |
| **90** | *Wa **uzlifati**'l-jannatu li'l-muttaqīn* | Those who guard themselves | **The garden** |

**The same root, toward two different ends.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

**ثَمَّ** — "there" (an adverb of place). It must not be confused with ***thumma*** ("then"); its vowelling and its meaning are different. **They occur not in the same verse but two verses apart:** *thamma* (64) and *thumma* (66).

### ٱلْءَاخَرِين — twice, two verbs

**The word is repeated in two verses, and with two different verbs:**

| Verse | Verb | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **64** | *Azlafnā … al-ākharīn* | **We brought near** |
| **66** | *Aghraqna'l-ākharīn* | **We drowned** |

**There is one verse in between: *wa anjaynā Mūsā wa man maʿahu ajmaʿīn* (65) — the saving.**

**The arrangement of the three verses is therefore this: bringing near → saving → drowning.** The saving stands in the middle; **the drowning is recounted only after the saving is complete.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the order.** And the same order is present in the section on Noah recorded in `037-saffat.md`: first the deliverance, then the end of the other side.

**أَجْمَعِين** — "all together". The word announces the completeness of the deliverance: **no one was left behind.**

### The sealing of the account — 67-68

**The second seal of the sūra. The mould and its function were treated in the section on 26/8-9; I do not repeat them.**

**The only thing to be added here is an observation about position:** the seal closes, at the end of the account of Moses, a telling of **fifty-seven verses** — the longest block in the sūra. **And the seal refers to no detail of the account:** *inna fī dhālika la-āyatan* — "in **this** there is a sign". What *dhālika* points to is not said.

**This is what makes it possible for the seal to remain the same eight times:** because the demonstrative is left indefinite, it can be set unchanged at the end of every account. **I record this as an observation about the structure.**

---

## The account of Abraham — 26/69-104 (introduction)

**Thirty-six verses.** And the second account that does not fit the mould of the sūra: no *kadhdhabat … al-mursalīn*, no *akhūhum*, no threefold introduction. **There is only the seal** (103-104).

**But the account itself does something different from all the other accounts of the sūra: at its end the fate of the people is not recounted.** Noah's people are drowned, ʿĀd is destroyed, Thamūd is seized, the people of Lot are wiped out, the people of al-Ayka are taken by the punishment of the shadow. **What happened to Abraham's people is not said.**

**In its place the account moves to the scene of the gathering (87-102).** It shows the punishment, that is, not in this world but in the next.

**I record this as an observation, and it can be verified from the text:** in six of the seven accounts the outcome is worldly; **in the account of Abraham the outcome is otherworldly.**

**The account divides into three parts:**

| Part | Verses | Subject |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | 69-77 | **The dispute** — the idols, the proof from hearing, the forefathers |
| **B** | 78-89 | **The confession and the prayer** — the five-sentence sequence and six petitions |
| **C** | 90-104 | **The scene of the gathering** — the garden, the fire, the dispute, the seal |

---

## 26/69-74 — وَٱتْلُ عَلَيْهِمْ نَبَأَ إِبْرَٰهِيمَ · إِذْ قَالَ لِأَبِيهِ وَقَوْمِهِۦ مَا تَعْبُدُونَ · قَالُوا۟ نَعْبُدُ أَصْنَامًا فَنَظَلُّ لَهَا عَٰكِفِينَ · قَالَ هَلْ يَسْمَعُونَكُمْ إِذْ تَدْعُونَ · أَوْ يَنفَعُونَكُمْ أَوْ يَضُرُّونَ · قَالُوا۟ بَلْ وَجَدْنَآ ءَابَآءَنَا كَذَٰلِكَ يَفْعَلُونَ

*Wa'tlu ʿalayhim nabaʾa Ibrāhīm · Idh qāla li-abīhi wa qawmihi mā taʿbudūn · Qālū naʿbudu aṣnāman fa-naẓallu lahā ʿākifīn · Qāla hal yasmaʿūnakum idh tadʿūn · Aw yanfaʿūnakum aw yaḍurrūn · Qālū bal wajadnā ābāʾanā kadhālika yafʿalūn*

"**Recite to them the tidings of Abraham as well.** · When he said to his father and his people: '**What is it you serve?**' · They said: '**We serve idols, and we keep standing before them.**' · He said: '**Do they hear you when you call? · Or do they bring you benefit or harm?**' · They said: '**No, but we found our forefathers doing so.**'"

### وَٱتْلُ — the addressee of the command

**The account opens with a command, and the addressee of the command is neither Moses nor Abraham but the one the sūra addresses: "recite to them".**

**The level of the telling therefore changes.** The account of Moses had begun *wa idh nādā rabbuka Mūsā* — direct narrative. **The account of Abraham begins with a command to deliver.**

**I record this as an observation:** the sūra sets the reason for recounting the account at the head of the account. **What is recounted is recounted in order to be recited to those addressed.**

**The root ت-ل-و** was analysed at `037-saffat.md` 37/3 and I do not repeat it. **The basic note there: the original sense of the root is not "to recite" but "to follow after";** the sense of reciting arises from setting letters one after another.

**نَبَأ** — root **ن-ب-أ**: **a great and weighty piece of news.** The root was analysed in `078-nebe.md`. **Ordinary news is called *khabar*; weighty news, *nabaʾ*.** And the word had also occurred in the sixth verse of the sūra (*anbāʾu mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn*).

### مَا تَعْبُدُونَ — the particle of the question, a second time

**And the second great question of the sūra is asked with the same particle as the first.**

| Verse | Who asks | To whom | Question |
|---|---|---|---|
| **23** | **Pharaoh** | To Moses | *Wa **mā** rabbu'l-ʿālamīn* |
| **70** | **Abraham** | To his people | ***Mā** taʿbudūn* |

**Both questions are asked with مَا.** And the difference must be recorded: in the twenty-third verse the use of *mā* was disputed, because what was asked about was a rational being. **Here there is no dispute:** what is asked about is stone, and *mā* is in place.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is that the two verses use the same particle:** the sūra sets up the same question in two different mouths. **One asks after the quiddity of the Lord and gets an answer; the other asks after the quiddity of the idols and gets none.**

**And a point of word order: Abraham does not say "whom do you serve?" but "what do you serve?"** The question asks not after the identity of what is served but after its **kind**.

### أَصْنَامًا فَنَظَلُّ لَهَا عَٰكِفِين — the answer itself

**And the answer is worth recording, because it contains no defence.**

**أَصْنَام** — the plural of *ṣanam*: **a carved, shaped idol.** The lexicographers transmit a distinction between *ṣanam* and *wathan*: *ṣanam* is what has been given a shape (in the form of a human or an animal), *wathan* a shapeless stone. **This distinction is not the same in every source; I make no firm choice.**

**And that the word comes indefinite must be recorded: *aṣnāman*.** No name is given, no number. **The answer, that is, does not identify what is served.**

**نَظَلُّ لَهَا عَٰكِفِين** — word for word: "we **keep bending over** them".

**ظَلَّ** — it had occurred in the fourth verse of the sūra too: *fa-**ẓallat** aʿnāquhum lahā khāḍiʿīn.* **The same verb, the same construction: *ẓalla* + *lahā* + a circumstantial word.**

| Verse | Expression | Who | What they do |
|---|---|---|---|
| **4** | *Fa-**ẓallat** aʿnāquhum **lahā** khāḍiʿīn* | **The necks** | They stay bowed before the sign |
| **71** | *Fa-**naẓallu lahā** ʿākifīn* | **The people** | They stay bent over the idols |

**The same verb, the same preposition, the same construction — to two different objects.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

**And as my own reading I record this:** what was recounted in the fourth verse was the result of a compulsion that does not come. **What is recounted in the seventy-first verse is an attachment made of one's own accord.** The sūra gives the same verb once to compulsion and once to choice. That this link is intended is not stated in the text.

**عَٰكِفِين** — root **ع-ك-ف**: **to stay in a place and not leave it, to remain beside a thing.** From the same root: *iʿtikāf* (staying in the mosque). **In the word there is not worship but *attachment and not departing*.**

**The people, that is, describe not the content of their worship but its duration: "we keep standing beside them."** I record this as an observation.

### هَلْ يَسْمَعُونَكُمْ إِذْ تَدْعُونَ — the proof from hearing

**This proof was treated in two places across the series, and I rely on those treatments:**

**`035-fatir.md` 35/13-14:** *in tadʿūhum lā yasmaʿū duʿāʾakum wa law samiʿū ma'stajābū lakum* — "if you call them they do not hear your call; and even if they heard, they could not answer you". **The structure recorded there had two stages: they do not hear; and even if they heard, they could not answer.**

**`046-ahkaf.md` 46/5-6:** *wa man aḍallu mimman yadʿū min dūni'llāhi man lā yastajību lahu ilā yawmi'l-qiyāmati wa hum ʿan duʿāʾihim ghāfilūn* — "who is further astray than one who calls, apart from God, upon those who cannot answer him until the Day of Resurrection? And they are unaware of their calling."

**Al-Shuʿarāʾ's difference must be recorded: here the proof is given not as a judgement but as a question.**

| Place | Mould | Who says it |
|---|---|---|
| **Fāṭir 35/14** | **Declarative** — "they do not hear" | God, to those addressed |
| **Al-Aḥqāf 46/5** | **Question + judgement** — "who is further astray?" | God, to those addressed |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/72-73** | **Question** — "do they hear?" | **Abraham, to his people** |

**In al-Shuʿarāʾ, then, the proof makes the other side supply the answer.** Abraham issues no judgement; **he asks.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the difference of mould in the three verses:** a messenger speaking inside an account leads the one he addresses to his own answer. **And the one addressed cannot give it** — the next verse will show this.

### Three verbs — hearing, benefit, harm

**The question contains three verbs, and their order is worth recording:**

| Order | Verb | What it asks |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Yasmaʿūnakum* | **Do they hear** — communication |
| 2 | *Yanfaʿūnakum* | **Do they bring benefit** |
| 3 | *Yaḍurrūn* | **Do they bring harm** |

**The object of the third verb has dropped: *yaḍurrūn* — "do they bring harm" (it does not say "you").** The first two verbs have the pronoun *kum*, the third does not.

**The transmitted explanation: it was elided for the sake of the verse-ending; the object is understood from the sentence.** This is an ordinary kind of ellipsis in Arabic, and similar examples were recorded in `055-rahman.md`.

**And the arrangement of the three verbs must be recorded:** the three supports of a relation of worship are set out — **being heard, gaining, fearing.** When all three are negated, the relation has no support left.

**And the word *duʿāʾ*:** *idh **tadʿūn*** — root **د-ع-و**: to call, to summon. **The word describes worship in its barest form: calling out.** And at the end of the sūra those addressed will be spoken to with the same root: *fa-lā **tadʿu** maʿa'llāhi ilāhan ākhar* (213). **This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.**

### بَلْ وَجَدْنَآ ءَابَآءَنَا كَذَٰلِكَ يَفْعَلُونَ — the proof from the forefathers

**And the answer is no answer to the question.**

**بَلْ** — in Arabic the particle of **retraction** (*iḍrāb*): leaving the preceding statement and passing to something else. **The people, that is, do not even say "no, they do not hear"; they change the subject.**

**I record this as an observation about the structure, and the particle shows it on its own:** *bal* is the particle for abandoning the answering of a question and passing to a new ground. **What was asked for was a proof; what is given is a custom.**

**The proof from the forefathers was treated in detail in two places across the series, and I rely on those treatments:**

**`043-zuhruf.md` 43/22-23:** *bal qālū innā wajadnā ābāʾanā ʿalā ummatin wa innā ʿalā āthārihim muhtadūn* (22) and *wa kadhālika mā arsalnā min qablika fī qaryatin min nadhīrin illā qāla mutrafūhā innā wajadnā ābāʾanā ʿalā ummatin wa innā ʿalā āthārihim muqtadūn* (23). **What was recorded there was this: the same sentence is repeated in two verses, and in the second it is said that this is an answer repeated in every age.**

**`031-lokman.md` 31/20-21:** *wa idhā qīla lahumu'ttabiʿū mā anzala'llāhu qālū bal nattabiʿu mā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā.* **There too the same particle *bal* stands.**

**What is peculiar to al-Shuʿarāʾ must be recorded:**

| Place | Expression | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Zukhruf 43/22** | *Ābāʾanā ʿalā **ummatin*** … *innā ʿalā āthārihim **muhtadūn*** | **"They were on a way and we are rightly guided"** — a claim to guidance is present |
| **Luqmān 31/21** | *Bal **nattabiʿu** mā wajadnā ʿalayhi ābāʾanā* | **"We follow it"** — the language of choice |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/74** | *Bal wajadnā ābāʾanā **kadhālika yafʿalūn*** | **"We found them doing so"** — observation only |

**And the difference is this: in the answer in al-Shuʿarāʾ there is no claim at all.** It is not said "we are rightly guided", nor "we follow them". **Only a fact is announced: "they used to do so."**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the comparison of the three verses:** the answer in al-Shuʿarāʾ is **the barest form** of the proof from the forefathers — mere continuity, with no ground. And Abraham's next words touch exactly on that bareness: *wa ābāʾukumu'l-**aqdamūn*** (76) — "**and your most ancient forefathers**".

---

## 26/75-77 — قَالَ أَفَرَءَيْتُم مَّا كُنتُمْ تَعْبُدُونَ · أَنتُمْ وَءَابَآؤُكُمُ ٱلْأَقْدَمُونَ · فَإِنَّهُمْ عَدُوٌّ لِّىٓ إِلَّا رَبَّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِينَ

*Qāla a-fa-raʾaytum mā kuntum taʿbudūn · Antum wa ābāʾukumu'l-aqdamūn · Fa-innahum ʿaduwwun lī illā rabba'l-ʿālamīn*

"He said: '**Have you then seen what you have been serving — · you and your most ancient forefathers? · They are an enemy to me; only the Lord of all the worlds is not.**'"

### أَفَرَءَيْتُم — the work of the pattern

**The pattern occurs often in the Qurʾān and was treated in this commentary in `053-necm.md` and `056-vakia.md`.** *A-raʾayta* — word for word "have you seen?", but its function is **"tell me, then, what do you say?"**. **It asks not for seeing with the eye but for thinking.**

**And the same pattern will occur once more in the sūra:** *a-fa-raʾayta in mattaʿnāhum sinīn* (205).

### ٱلْأَقْدَمُون — the reply to the proof from the forefathers

The root **ق-د-م**: **priority, being in front.** *Aqdam* — an elative: **older, oldest.**

**And the point of word order stands here. The people had said "our forefathers" (74); Abraham adds one word to the sentence: *al-aqdamūn* — "the most ancient".**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the added word:** the proof from the forefathers rests on **continuity**. Abraham does not reject the proof; **he extends it.** He carries the chain back to its very beginning. **Because the longer the chain grows, the sharper becomes the question of what the first link rests on.**

**And what was recorded at `043-zuhruf.md` 43/23 supports this reading:** there it was said that the same answer is given **in every age**. **The proof from the forefathers is thus a chain that produces itself; each generation cites the one before it as its ground, and the head of the chain is never asked after.**

### فَإِنَّهُمْ عَدُوٌّ لِّى — the direction of the enmity

**And the word order of the sentence must be recorded. Abraham does not say "I am an enemy to them"; he says "they are an enemy to me".**

| Construction | Its meaning |
|---|---|
| *Anā ʿaduwwun lahum* | **I** am the enemy — I am the subject |
| ***Innahum ʿaduwwun lī*** | **They** are the enemy — they are the subject |

**The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Ground |
|---|---|
| **Reciprocity is meant** | In Arabic enmity is two-sided; when one is said the other is understood |
| **The idols do harm** | What is worshipped, by leading its worshipper to the wrong place, has acted as an enemy to him |
| **They will be enemies at the gathering** | The Qurʾān says this openly: *wa kānū bi-ʿibādatihim kāfirīn* (al-Aḥqāf 46/6, treated in `046-ahkaf.md`) |

**The third explanation has the strongest ground within the Qurʾān** and accords with the sūra's own continuation as well: from the ninety-second verse onward it will be recounted that at the gathering those served are nowhere to be found.

**عَدُوّ** — the word comes in the singular but is the predicate of a plural group (*hum*). **In Arabic *ʿaduww* is used for both singular and plural** — it is a property of the *faʿūl* pattern; the same was said of *rasūl* in the sixteenth verse.

### إِلَّا رَبَّ ٱلْعَٰلَمِين — the kind of the exception

**And the exception at the end of the sentence has been disputed.**

**The tension is this: it is said "what you serve is an enemy to me, except the Lord of all the worlds". But the people were not serving the Lord of all the worlds.**

**The transmitted explanations:**

| Explanation | Kind | Ground |
|---|---|---|
| **A disjunctive exception** | An exception not of the same class | In Arabic *illā* can come in the sense of "but rather"; "they are my enemy — but the Lord of all the worlds is not" |
| **A conjunctive exception** | An exception of the same class | The people were not denying God altogether; they counted the idols as bringing them near to Him (as in *li-yuqarribūnā ila'llāhi zulfā*, al-Zumar 39/3) |

**Both explanations are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.** What is to be recorded is this: **from the next verse onward Abraham will describe with five sentences the party he has excepted.** Who the exception is, that is, is explained immediately after.

---

## 26/78-82 — ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَنِى فَهُوَ يَهْدِينِ · وَٱلَّذِى هُوَ يُطْعِمُنِى وَيَسْقِينِ · وَإِذَا مَرِضْتُ فَهُوَ يَشْفِينِ · وَٱلَّذِى يُمِيتُنِى ثُمَّ يُحْيِينِ · وَٱلَّذِىٓ أَطْمَعُ أَن يَغْفِرَ لِى خَطِيٓـَٔتِى يَوْمَ ٱلدِّينِ

*Alladhī khalaqanī fa-huwa yahdīn · Wa'lladhī huwa yuṭʿimunī wa yasqīn · Wa idhā mariḍtu fa-huwa yashfīn · Wa'lladhī yumītunī thumma yuḥyīn · Wa'lladhī aṭmaʿu an yaghfira lī khaṭīʾatī yawma'd-dīn*

"**It is He who created me, and He guides me. · It is He who feeds me and gives me drink. · When I fall ill, it is He who heals me. · It is He who will cause me to die and then bring me to life. · And it is He whose forgiveness of my fault on the Day of Judgement I hope for.**"

**The five densest verses of the sūra. Very short sentences, a single subject, five fields of action, and from beginning to end a single sound: *‑īn*.**

### The structure of the sequence — countable data

**Five sentences rest on the same mould, but the mould is not fixed. The differences are in the table, and all of them are read off the text:**

| # | Verse | Conjunction + relative | The separating pronoun *huwa* | Verb(s) | Time |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **78** | *(no wāw)* — ***alladhī*** | ***fa-huwa*** yahdīn | *khalaqa* (perfect) + *yahdī* (imperfect) | **Past + present** |
| 2 | **79** | *wa'lladhī* | ***huwa*** yuṭʿimunī | *yuṭʿimu* + *yasqī* (two imperfects) | **Present** |
| 3 | **80** | *(no relative)* — ***wa idhā*** | ***fa-huwa*** yashfīn | ***mariḍtu*** (perfect, **first person**) + *yashfī* | **Condition + result** |
| 4 | **81** | *wa'lladhī* | **none** | *yumītu* + *yuḥyī* | **Future** |
| 5 | **82** | *wa'lladhī* | **none** | *aṭmaʿu* (**first person**) + *yaghfira* | **The hereafter** |

**Four facts are read straight off the table:**

**One — in the first sentence there is no *wāw*.** The sequence begins *alladhī khalaqanī*; the following four are joined with *wa'lladhī*. **The first sentence is therefore not a member of the sequence but its head.** It stands as a direct attribute of *rabba'l-ʿālamīn* in the seventy-seventh verse.

**Two — in the third sentence there is no relative pronoun.** The other four are built with *alladhī* ("the one who"); the third with *wa idhā mariḍtu* ("when I fall ill"). **The mould breaks in the middle of the sequence.**

**Three — the separating pronoun (*huwa*) is present in the first three sentences and absent in the last two.**

**Four — a first-person verb occurs in only two places: *mariḍtu* (80) and *aṭmaʿu* (82).** The subject of every other verb is God; the subject of these two is Abraham.

### The order of the five fields

**The sentences are arranged in the order of a life, and this can be verified from the text:**

| # | Field | Axis of time |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Creation and guidance** | The beginning |
| 2 | **Eating and drinking** | Daily life |
| 3 | **Illness and healing** | The breakdown of life |
| 4 | **Death and resurrection** | The end and what follows |
| 5 | **Forgiveness** | The reckoning |

**And the sequence has a widening circle:** the first sentence takes **existence** as its subject, the second **the body**, the third **the body's failing**, the fourth **the body's ending**, the fifth **what comes after the body**.

**I record this as an observation:** the sequence is not a list of doctrines; **it is a person recounting his own life in order.** And this explains why the sentences are so concrete — there is no abstract attribute, all of them are verbs.

### The first sentence — ٱلَّذِى خَلَقَنِى فَهُوَ يَهْدِينِ

**Two verbs, two different tenses: *khalaqa* (perfect) and *yahdī* (imperfect).**

**And the *fāʾ* between them must be recorded.** *Fāʾ al-sababiyya* — the *fāʾ* that announces cause: **"He created me, and for that reason He guides me."** Guidance, that is, is presented as the continuation of creation: the one who creates does not leave what he created without a way.

**The same link is made elsewhere in the Qurʾān:** *alladhī aʿṭā kulla shayʾin khalqahu thumma hadā* (Ṭāhā 20/50) — "who gave everything its creation, then guided". **In both verses the order is the same: first creation, then the way.**

**And at `043-zuhruf.md` 43/26-27 a similar sentence occurs in Abraham's mouth:** *illā'lladhī faṭaranī fa-innahu **sa-yahdīn*** — "except the one who originated me; He will guide me". **The difference between the two verses is one letter:**

| Sūra | Expression | Mood |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Zukhruf 43/27** | *Fa-innahu **sa**-yahdīn* | ***Sīn*** — future |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/78** | *Fa-huwa yahdīn* | **Imperfect** — continuance |

**This is a difference that can be verified from the text.** And **as my own reading** I record this: in al-Zukhruf the sentence stands inside a declaration of **separation** from father and people, and with the future it announces a confidence; in al-Shuʿarāʾ the sentence stands at the head of a sequence of **description** and announces continuance. **Two contexts require two moods.** This is an explanation; it is not binding.

**And the verb *yahdīn* ties to the sixty-second verse of the sūra:** at the seashore Moses had said *inna maʿiya rabbī **sa-yahdīn***. **The same verb, the same pattern, two prophets.** The difference: Moses with the *sīn* (the near future), Abraham with *fa-huwa* (continuance). **This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.**

### The second sentence — وَٱلَّذِى هُوَ يُطْعِمُنِى وَيَسْقِينِ

**Two verbs: *yuṭʿimu* (He feeds) and *yasqī* (He gives to drink).**

**And the position of the separating pronoun changes.** In the first sentence it was *fa-huwa* (before the verb, with the *fāʾ*); here it is *huwa* directly. **In Arabic this pronoun (*ḍamīr al-faṣl*) announces restriction: "the one who feeds is He alone."**

**The concreteness of the words must be recorded:** the verse does not say "He provides" (*yarzuqu*). **It says "He feeds and He gives to drink."** Provision is an abstract concept; feeding and giving to drink are two acts of the hand.

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the choice of words:** the whole sequence is built on this concreteness. Abraham had told his people that the idols do not hear (72); **now he describes his own Lord by the most everyday of acts.** The comparison is made not through attributes but **through verbs**.

### The third sentence — وَإِذَا مَرِضْتُ فَهُوَ يَشْفِينِ

**The middle of the sequence, the sequence's one break, and the real subject of this commentary in this section.**

**The sentence differs from the other four in three respects:**

| Difference | The other four sentences | **The third sentence** |
|---|---|---|
| **Construction** | *(wa)'lladhī* — a relative | ***wa idhā*** — a conditional particle |
| **Subject of the verb** | God | ***I*** — *mariḍtu* |
| **Time** | Continuance or future | **Condition and answer** |

**And the main note is this: at one single place in the sequence the pronoun shifts to the first person, and it does so precisely in the sentence about illness.**

**In the four sentences the verbs are built thus:**

- *khalaqa-**nī*** — **He** created me
- *yuṭʿimu-**nī***, *yasqī-**n*** — **He** feeds me, gives me drink
- *yumītu-**nī***, *yuḥyī-**n*** — **He** causes me to die, brings me to life
- *yaghfira **lī*** — **He** forgives me

**But in the third sentence:**

- ***mariḍtu*** — **I** fell ill
- *yashfī-**n*** — **He** heals me

**Throughout the sequence, then, God is the agent and Abraham the object. In one verb alone Abraham becomes the agent — and that verb is illness.**

**I treat this difference as my own reading. I give its grounds in order.**

**The first ground — that the mould has been broken deliberately.** The sequence could have been built as *wa'lladhī yumriḍunī fa-huwa yashfīn* ("it is He who makes me ill and heals me"); the mould allowed for it, and the sentence about death and life (81) is in exactly this mould: *yumītunī **thumma** yuḥyīn.* **The sūra, that is, uses the same double-verb mould one verse later.** It does not use it in the sentence about illness.

**The second ground — that death and illness are handled separately.** In the fourth sentence death is attributed directly to God: *yumītunī*. **The text, that is, does not avoid attributing an act that appears negative (causing death).** The difference in the sentence about illness cannot therefore be explained by a general rule such as "what is unpleasant is not attributed" — because one verse later death is attributed.

**The third ground — the human-facing side of the causes of illness.** Illness, unlike death, passes through a person's acts, his negligence, his surroundings and his choices. Death comes to everyone and comes of necessity; **illness is a breakdown, and in the chain of causes of a breakdown the person too is present.**
