**The link the lexicographers make: the *ʿashīra* is the circle with which a person keeps *muʿāshara*, with whom he shares his daily life.** It announces, that is, closeness rather than descent.

**ٱلْأَقْرَبِين** — root **ق-ر-ب**, the plural of an elative: **the nearest.**

**And the construction narrows twice over:** *ʿashīra* is already the near circle; *al-aqrabīn* narrows it once more.

**And the comparison recorded at `070-mearic.md` 70/13 attaches here:** there the word *faṣīla* was treated (*wa faṣīlatihi'llatī tuʾwīh*) and it was recorded that **"al-Maʿārij's *faṣīla* announces a narrower circle"**. **I rely on that treatment.**

**And the gradation recorded in `074-muddessir.md` attaches here.** The note there was this: **the addressee of the warning is determined in the Qurʾān by degrees.** I repeat it in a table, because this verse is one link of that table:

| Verse | Addressee | Circle |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Muddaththir 74/2** | *Qum fa-andhir* — **no limit** | Indefinite |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/214** | *ʿAshīrataka'l-aqrabīn* | **The narrowest** |
| Al-Anʿām 6/92; al-Shūrā 42/7 | *Umma'l-qurā wa man ḥawlahā* | The town and its surroundings |
| Sabaʾ 34/28 | *Kāffatan li'n-nās* | **The widest** |

**And the discussion recorded at `071-nuh.md` 71/8-9 attaches here:** there the order in Noah's call (secret / open) was discussed and **this verse was cited as the ground for the "from the narrow circle to the wide" reading.** I rely on the note there; **there too no firm preference was adopted.**

**I record an observation, and it accords with the principle of this commentary:** the verse does not **exempt** the nearest circle; on the contrary it makes it **the first addressee**. **Closeness, that is, is not an exemption from warning but the priority of the warning.**

### وَٱخْفِضْ جَنَاحَكَ — lowering the wing

The root **خ-ف-ض**: **to lower, to bring down, to soften.** Its opposite is *rafʿ* (to raise). The Qurʾān uses the same root for lowering the voice as well (not *wa'ghḍuḍ min ṣawtika*, but in a similar field, Luqmān 31/19 — treated in `031-lokman.md`).

**جَنَاح** — **a wing.** Used of a human being it means **"the arm, the side"**.

**And the picture of the idiom is taken from the behaviour of birds:** when a bird wishes to protect its young or its mate, **it lowers its wing and takes it beneath**. A wing raised is ready for flight; lowered, it is ready for **protection**.

**The Qurʾān uses the same idiom once more:** *wa'khfiḍ lahumā janāḥa'dh-dhulli mina'r-raḥma* (al-Isrāʾ 17/24) — toward parents, **"lower to them the wing of humility out of mercy"**. **The same verb, the same word.**

### Two idioms of the wing — a comparison with al-Qaṣaṣ 28/32

**And another idiom of the same word was treated at `028-kasas.md` 28/29-35 and I rely on that treatment.**

**Al-Qaṣaṣ 28/32:** *wa'ḍmum ilayka janāḥaka mina'r-rahb* — "**draw your wing to yourself against fear**".

**The note there was this:** the word *janāḥ* used of a human being means "the arm, the side", and **the lexicographers explain this idiom as a person's collecting himself, coming out of a shudder; they base it on the picture of a bird spreading its wings in alarm and gathering them when calm.** And there this was transmitted as **"a connection the lexicographers make"**.

**Now I set the two idioms side by side:**

| | **Al-Qaṣaṣ 28/32** | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/215** |
|---|---|---|
| **Expression** | *Wa'ḍmum **ilayka** janāḥaka* | *Wa'khfiḍ janāḥaka **li-man** ittabaʿaka* |
| **Verb** | **ض-م-م** — to draw to oneself, to gather | **خ-ف-ض** — to lower, to bring down |
| **Direction** | ***Ilayka*** — **inward**, toward oneself | ***Li-man*** — **toward another** |
| **Cause** | *Mina'r-rahb* — **out of fear** | *Mina'l-muʾminīn* — **for those who follow** |
| **To whom** | **Moses** | **The messenger addressed** |
| **What it provides** | **Collecting oneself** | **Gentleness, protection** |

**The two idioms use the same word (*janāḥ*) but describe two different motions: one *gathers* the wing, the other *lowers* it.**

**And the difference stands in the prepositions:** *ilayka* (toward yourself) and *li-man ittabaʿaka* (for those who follow).

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the direction of the two verbs:** the motion in al-Qaṣaṣ is **turned inward** and quiets a fear; the motion in al-Shuʿarāʾ is **turned outward** and takes a community beneath it. **Two verses use the same limb for two different needs.** This is an explanation; it is not binding.

**And an observation about the structure: the addressee of the command is limited.** *Li-mani'ttabaʿaka **mina'l-muʾminīn*** — "to **those of the believers** who follow you". **The command, that is, orders a bearing not toward everyone but toward a particular group.** And the next verse gives a separate bearing for those who do not follow.

### فَإِنْ عَصَوْكَ فَقُلْ إِنِّى بَرِىٓءٌ — the language of separation

The root **ع-ص-ي**: **to oppose, to refuse to submit.** *ʿIṣyān* — the opposite of obedience (*ṭāʿa*).

The root **ب-ر-أ**: **to part from a thing, to come clear of it, to be free of it.** From the same root: *barāʾa* (standing clear, severing connection) and *barīʾ* (clear, unconnected).

**And a point of word order must be recorded, and it accords with the principle of the sūra:**

***Innī barīʾun mimmā taʿmalūn*** — "I am quit of **what you do**."

**The clearing is not from the persons but from the acts.** The verse does not say *innī barīʾun minkum* ("I am quit of you").

**And this has the same structure as Lot's sentence in the hundred and sixty-eighth verse:** *innī li-**ʿamalikum** mina'l-qālīn* — "I am one of those who detest **what you do**".

| Verse | Who | Expression | Object |
|---|---|---|---|
| **168** | **Lot** | *Li-ʿamalikum mina'l-qālīn* | ***ʿAmal*** |
| **216** | **The messenger addressed** | *Barīʾun mimmā taʿmalūn* | ***ʿAmal*** |

**This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.** And **as my own reading** I record this: the sūra sets up separation twice in the same form — **from the act, not from the person.** This is one of the plainest textual grounds for the principle recorded in `STYLE.md`.

**And the change of pronoun must be recorded:** the sentence begins *fa-in **ʿaṣawka*** (third person — "if they disobey you") and then continues *mimmā **taʿmalūn*** (second person — "what **you** do"). **In Arabic this is called *iltifāt* (a shift in the direction of address).**

**The transmitted explanation:** a declaration of separation is a word that must be said to the face; the address therefore passes from the third person to the second.

### وَتَوَكَّلْ عَلَى ٱلْعَزِيزِ ٱلرَّحِيم — the two names of the seal

**And the two hundred and seventeenth verse carries here the two names from the seal repeated eight times in the sūra.**

| Place | Expression | Work |
|---|---|---|
| **The seal** (9, 68, 104, 122, 140, 159, 175, 191) | *Wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-**ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*** | **A statement** — who He is |
| **217** | *Wa tawakkal ʿala'l-**ʿazīzi'r-raḥīm*** | **A command** — whom to trust |

**The attribute announced eight times is thus made, at the end, the ground of a command.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the sameness of the two constructions:** through the sūra it had been said "your Lord is the Mighty, the Merciful". **In the two hundred and seventeenth verse it is said "so put your trust in Him".** Eight seals are tied to a single command.

The root **و-ك-ل**: **to commit a matter to another, to appoint as agent.** Form V (*tawakkala*) — **to leave one's affair to someone.**

**The root was treated in `039-zumer.md` and at `065-talak.md` 65/3 and I do not repeat it.**

### ٱلَّذِى يَرَىٰكَ حِينَ تَقُومُ وَتَقَلُّبَكَ فِى ٱلسَّٰجِدِين — two states

**And the ground of the command is given by two states: *ḥīna taqūm* and *taqallubaka fi's-sājidīn*.**

**And both expressions have been disputed. I give the transmitted explanations in a table:**

| Expression | Reading 1 | Reading 2 |
|---|---|---|
| *Ḥīna taqūm* | **When you rise for prayer** | **When you rise to any task, when you stand up** |
| *Taqallubaka fi's-sājidīn* | **Your turning between bowing and prostration in prayer** | **Your passing from state to state among those who prostrate, your moving among them** |

**I do not impose a preference.** Both readings have a ground: the first rests on the word *sājidīn* suggesting prayer, the second on the general sense of the verbs *qāma* and *taqallub*.

**ت-ق-ل-ب** — root **ق-ل-ب** (analysed in the fiftieth verse: to turn over, to return). **Form V (*taqallaba*): to turn about continually, to pass from state to state.**

**And the root occurs three times in the sūra, and this repetition must be recorded:**

| Verse | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **50** | *Innā ilā rabbinā **munqalibūn*** | **The sorcerers** |
| **219** | *Wa **taqallubaka** fi's-sājidīn* | **The messenger addressed** |
| **227** | *Ayya **munqalabin yanqalibūn*** | **Those who do wrong** |

**The same root, three different places: those who know the place of return, the one who turns about among those who prostrate, those who do not know the place of return.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text; what it means is interpretation.

### إِنَّهُۥ هُوَ ٱلسَّمِيعُ ٱلْعَلِيم — the closing

**Two names: *al-Samīʿ* (the Hearing) and *al-ʿAlīm* (the Knowing).**

**And the five occurrences of the root *samʿ* in the sūra were tabulated in the section on 26/210-213.** What is to be added here is this: **in the two hundred and twelfth verse it was said that the satans are "kept far from the hearing"; in the two hundred and twentieth God is named "the Hearing".** Eight verses apart, the same root, two different directions.

**And the separating pronoun (*huwa*) must be recorded:** *innahu **huwa**'s-samīʿu'l-ʿalīm.* **The same pronoun was present in the eight seals** (*la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm*). **As it draws toward its close, that is, the sūra repeats the grammar of the seal as well.**

---

## 26/221-223 — هَلْ أُنَبِّئُكُمْ عَلَىٰ مَن تَنَزَّلُ ٱلشَّيَٰطِينُ · تَنَزَّلُ عَلَىٰ كُلِّ أَفَّاكٍ أَثِيمٍ · يُلْقُونَ ٱلسَّمْعَ وَأَكْثَرُهُمْ كَٰذِبُونَ

*Hal unabbiʾukum ʿalā man tanazzalu'sh-shayāṭīn · Tanazzalu ʿalā kulli affākin athīm · Yulqūna's-samʿa wa aktharuhum kādhibūn*

"**Shall I tell you upon whom the satans come down? · They come down upon every sinful fabricator. · They lend an ear; and most of them are liars.**"

### The mould of the question

**هَلْ أُنَبِّئُكُمْ** — "shall I tell you?" **The mould is used in the Qurʾān to draw attention** (*hal unabbiʾukum bi-sharrin min dhālika* — al-Māʾida 5/60; *hal adullukum ʿalā tijāratin* — al-Ṣaff 61/10, treated in `061-saff.md`).

**And a point of word order must be recorded: the sūra takes up the claim a second time, and this time it changes direction.**

| Verses | What it does |
|---|---|
| **210-212** | **Negative** — "the satans did not bring it down, they cannot" |
| **221-223** | **Positive** — "shall I tell you upon whom they do come down?" |

**The sūra, that is, first refutes and then gives the right address.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the structure of the two sections:** there are two ways of refuting a claim — **to say "it is not so"** and **to say "the truth is thus"**. The sūra does both, setting a ten-verse section of commands between them.

### أَفَّاكٍ أَثِيم — two attributes

**أَفَّاك** — root **أ-ف-ك**, **the *faʿʿāl* pattern: an intensive — one who does it continually and much.**

**The root was analysed at `045-casiye.md` 45/7 and I rely on that treatment.** The description there: **أ-ف-ك — to turn a thing from its direction, to overturn it.** And as was recorded there, the root was treated at `046-ahkaf.md` 46/11 and 46/28 as well (*ifkun qadīm*, *ifkuhum*).

**And al-Jāthiya 45/7 and al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/222 use the same construction:**

| Sūra | Construction | Context |
|---|---|---|
| **Al-Jāthiya 45/7** | *Waylun li-kulli **affākin athīm*** | **One who hears the verses and grows proud** |
| **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/222** | *Tanazzalu ʿalā kulli **affākin athīm*** | **The one the satans come down upon** |

**The two constructions are exactly the same, and the word *kulli* is common to both.** This is a datum that can be verified from the text.

**And the continuation of the note in `045-casiye.md` attaches here:** there what the *affāk* does next was recounted (*yasmaʿu āyāti'llāhi tutlā ʿalayhi thumma yuṣirru mustakbiran ka-an lam yasmaʿhā*). **In al-Shuʿarāʾ, what he receives is recounted.**

**أَثِيم** — root **أ-ث-م**: the core the lexicographers give is **to weigh down, to hold back** — *ithm* is the act that keeps a person from good and leaves him behind. **The *faʿīl* pattern: an assimilate adjective — settled, become a trait.**

**And the coming together of the two attributes must be recorded:**

| Attribute | Its field |
|---|---|
| ***Affāk*** | **Speech** — lying, turning about |
| ***Athīm*** | **Deed** — sin |

**The description, that is, has two wings: what he says and what he does.** And this is precisely the measure of the two hundred and twenty-sixth verse: *wa annahum yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn* — **the fit between what one says and what one does.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the fields of the two attributes.**

### يُلْقُونَ ٱلسَّمْعَ — the dispute over the pronoun

**And the disputed point in the verse is this: to whom do the pronouns of *yulqūna* and *aktharuhum* refer?**

| Reading | The pronoun | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **To the satans** | *Yulqūna's-samʿa* — they strain to listen | "The satans lend an ear [to heaven]; and most of them are liars" |
| **To those who deal with the *affāk*s** | Diviners / receivers of news | "They lend an ear [to the satans]; and most of them are liars" |

**Both readings are transmitted; I do not impose a preference.**

**And the tension with the two hundred and twelfth verse must be recorded:** there it was said *innahum ʿani's-samʿi la-**maʿzūlūn*** ("they are kept far from the hearing"). **Here it says *yulqūna's-samʿa* ("they lend an ear").**

**The transmitted explanation: lending an ear (*ilqāʾ al-samʿ*) is an *effort*; being kept away is the outcome.** That is, "they try to listen but cannot reach". **This explanation reconciles the two verses and accords with the mould of the two hundred and twelfth verse — there the passive was used.**

**ألقى السمع** — an idiom in Arabic: **to give one's ear, to be all attention.** The Qurʾān uses the same idiom once more: *aw alqa's-samʿa wa huwa shahīd* (Qāf 50/37 — treated in `050-kaf.md`). **The same idiom, in two different contexts.**

**And the verb *alqā* must be recorded: the root ل-ق-ي occurred six times in the sūra in the scene of the sorcerers** (43-46; tabulated in the section on 26/43-49). **The sūra, that is, uses the verb "to cast/to throw" in the scene of sorcery and in the discussion of the satans.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.

**وَأَكْثَرُهُمْ كَٰذِبُون** — "and most of them are liars."

**And the word *akthar* (most) must be recorded: it is the same word as in the eight seals of the sūra** — *wa mā kāna **aktharuhum** muʾminīn.*

| Place | Expression | Who |
|---|---|---|
| **The eight seals** | *Wa mā kāna **aktharuhum** muʾminīn* | **The peoples** |
| **223** | *Wa **aktharuhum** kādhibūn* | **Those who lend an ear** |

**The same word, the same construction.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text. **And in neither is "all of them" said.**

---

## 26/224-226 — وَٱلشُّعَرَآءُ يَتَّبِعُهُمُ ٱلْغَاوُۥنَ · أَلَمْ تَرَ أَنَّهُمْ فِى كُلِّ وَادٍ يَهِيمُونَ · وَأَنَّهُمْ يَقُولُونَ مَا لَا يَفْعَلُونَ

*Wa'sh-shuʿarāʾu yattabiʿuhumu'l-ghāwūn · A-lam tara annahum fī kulli wādin yahīmūn · Wa annahum yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn*

"**As for the poets, those gone astray follow them. · Do you not see that they wander bewildered in every valley · and that they say what they do not do?**"

### The section that gives the sūra its name

**Three verses. And the sūra takes its name from the first of these three: *al-shuʿarāʾ*.**

**And the position of the section must be recorded: since the two hundred and twenty-first verse the sūra has taken the source of speech as its subject.** First upon whom the satans come down (221-223), then the poets (224-226), then the exception (227).

**I record this as an observation about the context:** the section is not an independent "discussion of poetry" but **the last link of an answer given to a claim.** The claim the sūra answers was this: "this man is a poet, his speech is poetry." **And the Qurʾān touches on this claim in many sūras** — *bal qālū aḍghāthu aḥlāmin bali'ftarāhu bal huwa shāʿir* (al-Anbiyāʾ 21/5), *wa mā huwa bi-qawli shāʿir* (al-Ḥāqqa 69/41 — treated in `069-hakka.md`), *am yaqūlūna shāʿirun natarabbaṣu bihi rayba'l-manūn* (al-Ṭūr 52/30 — treated in `052-tur.md`).

### ش-ع-ر — the analysis of the root

**The core sense of the root is not "poetry".**

The root **ش-ع-ر**: **to sense, to become aware of something fine, hidden or not easily noticed.**

| Derivative | Its sense | Shared core |
|---|---|---|
| *Shaʿara* | He became aware, he sensed | **Perceiving** |
| *Shuʿūr* | Consciousness, awareness | **Perceiving** |
| *Shaʿr* | Hair | **What is fine** |
| *Mashʿar* | A sign, a mark; a place where a marker is set (*al-mashʿari'l-ḥarām*) | **What makes one aware** |
| *Shāʿir* | **One who perceives fine things and says them** | **Perceiving** |
| *Shiʿr* | Poetry | **Fine speech** |

**By the sense of the root, then, a *shāʿir* is not "one who makes rhymes" but *one who senses what is fine and says it*.** This is the dictionary value of the word, and it carries no negative sense.

**And the root had occurred twice more in the sūra, and this must be recorded:**

| Verse | Expression | Context |
|---|---|---|
| **113** | *Law **tashʿurūn*** | **Noah** — "if only you understood" |
| **202** | *Wa hum lā **yashʿurūn*** | **The punishment** — without their being aware |
| **224** | *Wa'**sh-shuʿarāʾ**u* | **The poets** |

**The sūra, that is, has used the root from which it takes its name twice in the sense of "to perceive", and in the third place as the name of a group.** This is a repetition that can be verified from the text.

### Word order — the structure of the sentence says a great deal

**The word order of the two hundred and twenty-fourth verse must be recorded, because the judgement comes out of it.**

***Wa'sh-shuʿarāʾu — yattabiʿuhumu'l-ghāwūn***

| Element | Word | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| **Subject** | *Al-shuʿarāʾu* | **The poets** |
| **Predicate (a verbal clause)** | *Yattabiʿuhumu'l-ghāwūn* | **Those gone astray follow them** |

**The sentence, that is, gives no attribute directly about the poets.** It does not say "poets are liars" or "poets are astray". **What it says is *who goes after them*.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the construction of the sentence:** the predicate is not about the poets themselves but **about their followers.** This also determines how the next two verses (225-226) are to be understood — because those two verses are attached by the pronoun *annahum*, and the referent of the pronoun is disputed.

### ٱلْغَاوُۥن — the place of the word within the sūra

**The root was analysed in `053-necm.md`, and its three occurrences in the sūra (91, 94, 224) were tabulated in the section on 26/90-95; I do not repeat it.**

**What is to be added here is this:** the word occurs the first two times **in the scene of the gathering in the account of Abraham**, and the third time here. **The sūra, that is, uses the word that names the side to whom the fire is shown, at the end, for a group of followers.**

**This is a repetition that can be verified from the text; what it means is interpretation, and two readings are possible:**

| Reading | Its meaning |
|---|---|
| **The word points to the same group** | The *ghāwūn* at the gathering and those who follow the poets bear the same attribute |
| **The word announces only the attribute** | In both places the attribute "gone from the way" is meant; they need not be the same persons |

**I do not impose a preference.**

### فِى كُلِّ وَادٍ يَهِيمُون — root ه-ي-م

**The root ه-ي-م** was analysed at `056-vakia.md` 56/55 and I rely on that treatment. The description there: ***hīm* is the plural of *ahyam*/*haymāʾ* and means a camel afflicted with a disease that leaves it never satisfied by water. *Huyām* — a condition found in camels that makes the animal drink continually while its thirst never passes.**

**And what was recorded there was this: the work of the comparison is to give not a measure but a *state* — a thirst that does not pass with drinking.**

**What is to be added here is the verb itself.**

The senses the lexicographers give for the verb **يَهِيمُ**:

| Sense | Ground |
|---|---|
| **To roam loose from thirst** | The root's core concerning camels |
| **To walk aimlessly, bewildered** | The widespread use |
| **To be beside oneself in love** | *Hayamān* — to lose one's reason in love |

**The core shared by the three senses must be recorded: *to move without being bound to a direction and without reaching satisfaction*.**

**And the expression *fī kulli wādin* ("in every valley") must be recorded.** **و-د-ي** — a valley: the hollow between two rises, a watercourse.

**The explanation the lexicographers give: *wādī* is used in Arabic figuratively for "a subject, a field" as well.** The English "to range over every subject" is close to it.

**The construction can therefore be understood in two ways, and both are transmitted:**

| Reading | Its meaning |
|---|---|
| **Literal** | To roam valley by valley — to be unsettled, to be loose |
| **Figurative** | To enter every subject — to stay nowhere, to have no consistent field |

**And both readings accord with the core of the verb *yahīmūn*: directionlessness.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the picture the root of the word and the construction build together:** what is criticised is not speaking but **the speech's not being bound to a direction.** The *hīm* camel is not one that does not drink — **it drinks and is not satisfied.** The matter, that is, is not inactivity but **the absence of satisfaction and of direction**.

### وَأَنَّهُمْ يَقُولُونَ مَا لَا يَفْعَلُون — the setting of the measure

**And the centre of gravity of this three-verse section is here.**

**The sentence is short and complete: *yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn* — "they say what they do not do."**

**And the same measure is set up once more in the Qurʾān, this time directly upon the believers. It was treated at `061-saff.md` 61/2-3 and I rely on that treatment.**

**Al-Ṣaff 61/2-3:** *yā ayyuha'lladhīna āmanū lima taqūlūna mā lā tafʿalūn · kabura maqtan ʿinda'llāhi an taqūlū mā lā tafʿalūn* — "You who believe! Why do you say what you do not do? It is greatly hateful in the sight of God that you should say what you do not do."

**I set the two verses side by side:**

| | **Al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/226** | **Al-Ṣaff 61/2-3** |
|---|---|---|
| **About whom** | **The poets** — third person | **The believers** — second person |
| **Mood** | *Yaqūlūna* — **statement** | *Taqūlūna* — **question and reproach** |
| **Verb** | *Mā lā **yafʿalūn*** | *Mā lā **tafʿalūn*** |
| **Added ruling** | **none** | *Kabura maqtan ʿinda'llāh* — **"greatly hateful in the sight of God"** |
| **What it does** | **Description** | **Prohibition** |

**And what the two verses build together must be recorded, and this is my own reading:**

**Al-Shuʿarāʾ describes a group by this attribute. Al-Ṣaff forbids the same attribute to the believers.** **The measure, that is, is not peculiar to one group.** The fit between what one says and what one does is in al-Shuʿarāʾ a measure of description, in al-Ṣaff a command.

**And this measure is set up within the sūra itself as well:** five messengers said five times *"I ask you no wage"* (109, 127, 145, 164, 180) — **and the sūra gives this not as a claim but as a record of conduct.** And the attribute of the messengers was *amīn* (107, 125, 143, 162, 178): **trustworthy, that is, one whose word and state hold together.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is the sūra as a whole:** from beginning to end the sūra separates the messenger and the other side by **the same measure** — **the distance between speech and act.** The two hundred and twenty-sixth verse names that measure.

### The referent of the pronoun — about whom are 225-226

**And this is the most important grammatical question of the section: to whom does the pronoun *annahum* in the two hundred and twenty-fifth and twenty-sixth verses refer?**

| Reading | The pronoun | Its meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **To the poets** | *Al-shuʿarāʾ* | "The poets wander in every valley and say what they do not do" |
| **To those gone astray (the followers)** | *Al-ghāwūn* | "Those who go after them wander in every valley and say what they do not do" |

**The first reading is the widespread one, and the exception in the verse that follows (227) supports it:** the exception is in the form *illā'lladhīna āmanū*, and it is understood **to set apart a portion of the poets.**

**The second reading is also transmitted, and its ground is that the last word of the two hundred and twenty-fourth verse is *al-ghāwūn* — a pronoun refers to the nearest noun.**

**I do not impose a preference.** What is to be recorded is this: **whichever reading is taken, the two hundred and twenty-seventh verse sets an exception and prevents the description from being generalised to a whole group.**

---

## 26/227 — إِلَّا ٱلَّذِينَ ءَامَنُوا۟ وَعَمِلُوا۟ ٱلصَّٰلِحَٰتِ وَذَكَرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا وَٱنتَصَرُوا۟ مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا ظُلِمُوا۟ ۗ وَسَيَعْلَمُ ٱلَّذِينَ ظَلَمُوٓا۟ أَىَّ مُنقَلَبٍ يَنقَلِبُونَ

*Illā'lladhīna āmanū wa ʿamilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāti wa dhakaru'llāha kathīran wa'ntaṣarū min baʿdi mā ẓulimū; wa sa-yaʿlamu'lladhīna ẓalamū ayya munqalabin yanqalibūn*

"**Except those who believe and do righteous deeds and remember God much and defend themselves after being wronged. Those who do wrong will soon know to what place of return they will return.**"

### The place of the exception — this verse must be brought forward

**The last verse of the sūra, and the most easily overlooked verse of the sūra.**

**The three-verse description (224-226) is limited by this verse. And the limitation determines how the description is to be understood.**

**I record this plainly, and it is the main ruling of this section:**

**The verse does not take poetry as its subject. It takes an *attitude* as its subject.**

**I give its grounds in order, and all of them are from the text:**

**The first ground — the exception itself.** If what were criticised were the art of poetry or being a poet, the exception would have come in the form **"except those who give up composing poetry"**. **It does not.** The exception names four attributes, and none of the four **has to do with poetry**: faith, righteous deeds, much remembrance, defending oneself after being wronged. **The distinction, that is, is built not on a trade or an art but on an attitude.**

**The second ground — the measure of verse 226.** The attitude criticised is named: *yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn* — **not doing what one says.** **This is not a fault belonging to poetry;** it can be found in every kind of speech. **And at al-Ṣaff 61/2-3 the same fault is forbidden to the believers** — and there is no poetry there.

**The third ground — the word order of verse 224.** As was recorded above, the predicate of the sentence is not an attribute about the poets but **a piece of information about their followers.** *Yattabiʿuhumu'l-ghāwūn.*

**The fourth ground — the verb of verse 225.** *Yahīmūn* — directionlessness. **Directionlessness is a fault not in the form of the speech but in what the speech is bound to.**

**On these four grounds I record the following distinction, and I draw the limit of the verse plainly as well:**

| What the verse says | What the verse does not say |
|---|---|
| An attitude is described: directionlessness and the mismatch of speech and act | **"Poetry is bad"** |
| Those who go after this attitude are *ghāwūn* | **"Poets are unbelievers"** |
| Poets who believe, do righteous deeds and defend themselves against wrong are outside this | **"Art is forbidden"** |

**And no general ruling about poetry or art is issued in this commentary.** The verse issues no ruling; **it describes an attribute and plainly leaves outside it those who do not bear that attribute.** This accords exactly with the principle recorded in `STYLE.md`: **a judgement is not built wholesale upon a group; it is the attributes the verse describes, and whoever bears that attribute is included in it.**

### The four attributes

| Order | Attribute | Its field |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | *Āmanū* | **Faith** |
| 2 | *ʿAmilu'ṣ-ṣāliḥāt* | **Deed** |
| 3 | *Dhakaru'llāha kathīrā* | **Remembrance** |
| 4 | *Wa'ntaṣarū min baʿdi mā ẓulimū* | **Defence against wrong** |

**The first three are a triad that occurs often in the Qurʾān.** The fourth is peculiar to this construction and deserves separate treatment.

**وَذَكَرُوا۟ ٱللَّهَ كَثِيرًا** — "**they remembered God much.**"

**And the setting of this attribute here must be recorded. I give it as my own reading:** the fault described in the two hundred and twenty-fifth verse was **directionlessness** (*fī kulli wādin yahīmūn*). **The counterpart of that is exactly remembrance: binding speech to a place.** The word *kathīran* (much) also announces continuance. **The third condition of the exception, that is, stands as the counterpart of the fault in the second verse.** That this link is intended is not stated in the text.

### وَٱنتَصَرُوا۟ مِنۢ بَعْدِ مَا ظُلِمُوا۟ — the fourth attribute

**The root ن-ص-ر** was analysed in the ninety-third verse, and it was recorded that Form VIII (*intaṣara*) adds **reflexivity**: **to defend oneself, to take one's own right.**

| Verse | Expression | Who | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| **93** | *Aw **yantaṣirūn*** | **The idols** | **They cannot** |
| **227** | *Wa'**ntaṣarū** min baʿdi mā ẓulimū* | **Those who believe** | **They do** |

**The same form, the same root, two different sides.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text, and it binds the two ends of the sūra.

**And two notes must be recorded, and both are in the sentence itself:**

**One — the defence comes *afterwards*.** *Min baʿdi mā ẓulimū* — "**after being wronged**". **The sentence, that is, describes not striking first but answering.**

**Two — the verb is passive: *ẓulimū* — "they were wronged".** Who did the wrong is not said; **the subject is the side to whom the wrong was done.**

**And this field was treated in `042-sura.md` and I rely on that treatment.** There the same root occurs: *wa'lladhīna idhā aṣābahumu'l-baghyu hum **yantaṣirūn*** (al-Shūrā 42/39), and immediately after a limit is set: *wa jazāʾu sayyiʾatin sayyiʾatun mithluhā* (42/40) — **that the requital be equal to the like of it.** I rely on the note there and do not repeat it here.

**What is to be recorded is this: al-Shuʿarāʾ 26/227 counts defence as an attribute; al-Shūrā 42/40 gives its measure.**

**And a note on the time of this verse's revelation:** as was stated in the introductory section of the sūra, **some sources transmit that the final verses are Medinan**, and this very expression is given as the ground. **I adopt no firm preference.**

### وَسَيَعْلَمُ ٱلَّذِينَ ظَلَمُوٓا۟ — the last sentence of the sūra

**And the sūra returns to where it began.**

**In the third verse the messenger had been told *"you are almost destroying yourself because they will not believe"*.** **In the two hundred and twenty-seventh verse it is said to whom the outcome belongs: *wa sa-yaʿlamu…* — "they will soon know".**

**سَ** (*sīn*) — the near future. **And in the sixth verse of the sūra the same particle did the same work:** *fa-**sa**-yaʾtīhim anbāʾu mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn.*

| Verse | Expression | Position |
|---|---|---|
| **6** | *Fa-**sa**-yaʾtīhim anbāʾu…* | **At the beginning of the sūra** |
| **227** | *Wa **sa**-yaʿlamu'lladhīna ẓalamū…* | **At the end of the sūra** |

**The two verses announce the same thing with the same particle: the outcome is deferred but not distant.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

### أَىَّ مُنقَلَبٍ يَنقَلِبُون — the last turning of the root

**The root was analysed in `084-insikak.md`, and this verse was already cited there; I rely on that treatment.** The note there: **ق-ل-ب — to turn over; *inqalaba* means both "to return" and "to arrive at one's end", and the Qurʾān uses this verse in the second sense.**

**What is to be added here is the repetition within the sūra. The root occurs three times in the sūra, and all three were tabulated (in the section on 26/214-220).** Here is the third and last occurrence.

**And the word order itself must be recorded: there is a cognate accusative, qualified by an interrogative.**

*Ayya **munqalabin** **yanqalibūn***

| Element | Word | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| Interrogative adjective | *Ayya* | **"Which"** |
| Cognate accusative | *Munqalaban* | **A place of return / a returning** |
| Verb | *Yanqalibūn* | **They will return** |

**The sentence, that is, does not say "where will they go"; it says "with what returning will they return".** And *munqalab* in Arabic can be both **a place of return** (a noun of place) and **a returning** (a verbal noun). **Both readings are transmitted.**

**And the word order of the sentence leaves a threat open:** **what** the place of return is is not said. It is only said that "they will know".

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is its link with the fourth verse of the sūra:** the sūra had begun "if We willed We would send down a sign from heaven", and it did not send it down. **At the end too it does not state the content of the threat.** **The sūra closes, that is, where it opened: information is not given, the coming of the information is announced.**

### What this says to the present

**The four verses from the two hundred and twenty-fourth to the two hundred and twenty-seventh set up a measure for how a field of speech is to be assessed, and the measure does not look to the kind of art.**

**The measure consists of three questions, and all three can be drawn from the text:**

| Question | Its counterpart in the verse |
|---|---|
| **Is the speech bound to a direction, or flung in every direction?** | *Fī kulli wādin yahīmūn* |
| **Does the speaker do what he says?** | *Yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn* |
| **Who goes after him?** | *Yattabiʿuhumu'l-ghāwūn* |

**And these three questions look today to a field far beyond poetry:** every kind of production of speech — writing, speaking, the platform, the performance, every kind of public expression. **The verse describes not a craft but a responsibility.**

**And the exception establishes the justice of the measure:** one who does the same work does not fall under the same judgement if he does not bear the same attributes. **This is the method the sūra applies from beginning to end** — seven peoples were recounted not by their names but by **what they did**; and in the eight seals it was not "all" but **"most"** that was said.

---

## The sūra as a whole — looking back

**In two hundred and twenty-seven verses al-Shuʿarāʾ does one thing: it recounts the same event seven times and ends each time with the same sentence.**

This is not a literary choice but **a form of proof.** The sūra does not prove a claim; **it shows a pattern.** And the method by which the pattern is shown is the sūra itself: **repetition.**

### The question the sūra answers

In the third verse the sūra observes a condition: **the messenger is consuming himself because those he addresses do not believe.** And the rest of the sūra gives three separate answers to this condition:

| Answer | Where | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| **Compulsion was possible; it was not chosen** | **4** | The outcome is not a matter standing at the limit of power |
| **Those before you received the same answer** | **10-191** | Seven accounts, seven times the same outcome |
| **The matter is not an inadequacy of expression** | **192-199** | Had the language changed, had the carrier changed, the outcome would not have changed |

**And what the three answers have in common is this: not one of them tells the messenger "work harder".** All three say the same thing: **the outcome is not your business.**

### The eight seals — what they do

**The most easily verifiable datum of the sūra is the two-verse seal repeated exactly eight times** (8-9, 67-68, 103-104, 121-122, 139-140, 158-159, 174-175, 190-191).

**And the two verses of the seal do two different pieces of work:**

| Verse | Expression | Its work |
|---|---|---|
| **The first** | *Inna fī dhālika la-āyatan wa mā kāna aktharuhum muʾminīn* | **The fact** — there was a sign, most did not believe |
| **The second** | *Wa inna rabbaka la-huwa'l-ʿazīzu'r-raḥīm* | **The measure** — the outcome is tied to two names |

**And in the two hundred and seventeenth verse these two names are tied to a command: *wa tawakkal ʿala'l-ʿazīzi'r-raḥīm*.**

**I record this as my own reading, and its ground is that eight repetitions and one command use the same construction:** eight times the sūra announces an attribute, and at the end it makes that attribute the ground of a bearing. **The seal, then, is not a closing formula; it is the preparation for the command to be given at the end of the sūra.**

### The measure of the sūra — speech and act

**From beginning to end the sūra uses a single measure, and in the two hundred and twenty-sixth verse it names that measure: *yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn*.**

**The application of the measure within the sūra is in the table, and all of it is textual data:**

| Who | Speech | Act | Fit |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The five messengers** | *Innī lakum rasūlun **amīn*** | *Mā asʾalukum ʿalayhi min ajr* (5 times) | **Present** |
| **Pharaoh** | *Ilāhan ghayrī* (29) | *Fa-mādhā taʾmurūn* (35) | **Absent** |
| **The sorcerers (before)** | *Innā la-naḥnu'l-ghālibūn* (44) | They were defeated (45) | **Absent** |
| **The sorcerers (after)** | *Lā ḍayra* (50) | They turned in spite of the threat | **Present** |
| **ʿĀd** | *Wa mā naḥnu bi-muʿadhdhabīn* (138) | *Fa-ahlaknāhum* (139) | **Absent** |
| **Abraham** | The five-sentence sequence (78-82) | He parted from his people (77) | **Present** |
| **The section on the poets** | *Yaqūlūna mā lā yafʿalūn* (226) | — | **Absent** |

**The sūra, that is, separates the parties not by their beliefs but by the distance between their speech and their acts.** This is an overlap that can be verified from the text.

### The two ends of the sūra — the opening and the closing

| | **The opening (1-9)** | **The closing (221-227)** |
|---|---|---|
| **Subject** | The Book itself | **The things that are not the source of the Book** |
| **To the messenger** | *Laʿallaka bākhiʿun nafsak* (3) | *Wa tawakkal* (217) |
| **Time** | *Fa-sa-yaʾtīhim* (6) | *Wa sa-yaʿlamu* (227) |
| **Information** | Not given — "the tidings will come" | Not given — "which return, they will know" |
| **The seal** | **8-9** — the first stamping | **Absent** — the sūra ends unsealed |

**The last row must be recorded: the sūra does not use at its end the seal it used eight times.**

**I record this as my own reading, and it is not binding:** the seal was set after something that closes — a proof, seven accounts. **The final section of the sūra, however, does not close:** its last sentence is left to a future (*wa sa-yaʿlamu*). **The absence of the seal accords with that waiting.** This is an explanation, not a ruling.

### The roots analysed in the sūra and where they were treated

**The table below is a list of the roots analysed here for the first time or treated in reliance on other sections of the series.**

| Root | Word | First time in this section? | Section relied on |
|---|---|---|---|
| **ب-خ-ع** | *bākhiʿ* (3) | **Yes** | — |
| **ب-خ-س** | *tabkhasū* (183) | No | `083-mutaffifin.md` |

### Closing

The last sentence of the sūra gives no information: *"they will **soon know** to what place of return they will return."* The information is deferred, but the deferral is not indefinite — the letter *sīn* announces the near future.

**And between the first sentence and the last sentence of the sūra lie two hundred and twenty-five verses; both do the same thing: they announce that something is coming and do not say what it is.**

Everything in between — seven prophets, eight seals, three sentences repeated five times, the sorcerers whose side changed in two minutes, houses carved out of mountains, the measure and the balance, and the measure of speech and act in the last three verses — **is set between these two announcements.**

And the one thing the sūra says to the messenger does not change from the third verse to the two hundred and seventeenth: **the burden you carry is delivery; it is not you who carries the outcome.**
